#134. South Sudan pushes back elections amid violent flare-ups

South Sudan has been independent from Sudan since 2011, and has not yet held national elections. Since then, the country faces cycles of violence after a civil war led to a tenuous peace deal.

South Sudan on Saturday pushed back its first national elections from December to February next year, as the country faces flare-ups of violence and political strife.

Presidential and legislative elections are now slated for February 11, 2027.

"We didn't postpone the elections. We have just adjusted the time to allow other processes to be implemented and that is according to the law," National Elections Commission spokeswoman and Commissioner Josephine Achiro said.

"Article 57 of the National Elections Commission gives us authority to adjust the elections timelines," she added.

Achiro said the postponement of the election date is for voter registration and civic education purposes.

According to the World Bank, the vast majority of South Sudanese lack state-recognized ID, creating challenges for voter registration. Thousands of centers have been established across the country to register voters.

South Sudan became independent from Sudan in July 2011 following a referendum. The country has some 12 million people and is one of the impoverished nations on the planet.

From 2013 to 2018, forces backed by President Silva Kiir, a member of the Dinka ethnic group, fought against troops supported by Vice President Riek Machar, who hails from the Nuer tribe. More than 400,000 people died in that conflict.

A fragile peace agreement was achieved between the two sides in 2018, with a national unity government established in 2020.

Kiir dissolved South Sudan's transitional government in September ahead of elections, and has since ruled by decree in a caretaker administration. He fired Vice President Machar, who had earlier been charged with treason and is under house arrest.

Despite the peace deal, there are also frequent clashes between the Dinka and Nuer groups loyal to Kiir and Machar.

The head of the UN Mission in South Sudan, Anita Kiki Ghebo, said earlier in the week that violence is increasing and added that approximately 10 million people in South Sudan need some form of humanitarian assistance. She also noted that 1.4 million people have entered South Sudan due to an ongoing conflict in Sudan.

"If left unaddressed, political, security, and humanitarian challenges risk reinforcing one another, further exacerbating tensions adn vulnerabilities across the country," she said.

She has urged renewed dialogue "to focus on establishing clear and credible roadmap for completing the outstanding provisions of the Revitalized Peace Agreement," the formal name for the 2018 peace deal.

Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps says it has targeted and destroyed a US early-warning radar in Bahrain in response to a US airstrike on a communications tower in Sirik overlooking the strait of Hormuz. It is Iran’s first direct attack on US military installations for months.

“This is a serious warning to the aggressor enemy to end its aerial mischief and oil smuggling in the strait of Hormuz,” the IRGC said. “In the event of repeated violations and aggression, the IRGC aerospace force is on full alert to confront the enemy without constraint.”

US Central Command denied that the radar system had been destroyed and said US forces had intercepted two Iranian drones headed for Bahrain.

Donald Trump has said in a series of media interviews over the past 24 hours that he will attack Iran only if necessary, claiming that Gulf countries, such as Saudi Arabia, have urged him to hold back from planned large-scale military strikes.

Trump said he had asked the vice-president, JD Vance, to end his interest in negotiating a new deal with Iran and instead focus on corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance had been seen by the Iranians as the most trustworthy figure close to Trump, but they had rejected his request for a personal meeting with their negotiators to discuss the terms of a peace deal.

Vance has been at the forefront of Republicans saying the war was the cause of the widespread Republican losses in the midterms. Trump said he had been disappointed in the American public because higher diesel prices were a small price to pay for preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

An opinion poll in the UK showed 65% of the British public would oppose the US resuming the war in Iran, and only 14% would back it. The poll was conducted by YouGov for the Council for Arab-British Understanding (Caabu).

It also found 54% oppose UK bases being used by the US to mount attacks on Iran, with 29% in favour. Support for additional UK sanctions against Iran was 45%, with 22% opposed.

The poll was conducted on Tuesday and Wednesday this week, before two Iranians were found guilty on Thursday of spying and planning attacks on journalists in the UK. The Iranian embassy has rejected the court judgment as an attempt to advance a political agenda.

Chris Doyle, the director of Caabu, said: “The British public are sceptical about any war on Iran and what it might achieve. What might make the Burnham government take note is the very high level of opposition to any further war amongst 2024 Labour voters, at 79%.”

Iran’s Assembly of Experts, a body of elected Islamic jurists responsible for choosing the supreme leader, has become the first governing institution in Iran to call for a review of the country’s defence doctrine, potentially leading to religious permission for Iran to try to acquire a nuclear weapon.

In a statement released on Thursday, the assembly called for a review of Iran’s membership of the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, describing the move as an “unavoidable necessity” and a “public and national demand”.

The intervention carries a religious as much as political significance because the 88-strong assembly comprises senior clerics, most of whom are supposed to be qualified to exercise independent Islamic legal reasoning.

Iran has had a longstanding prohibition on nuclear weapons, based on a fatwa issued by the late supreme leader Ali Khamenei, but the relevance of the fatwa in the wake of the US attacks on Iran has been questioned widely in political and security circles.

Whether Iran has the stockpiles of uranium or technology to build a nuclear weapon is contested.

The UN nuclear inspectorate has expressed alarm about a continued lack of information from Iran about its nuclear sites. We do not know the exact location of the Isfahan uranium enrichment facility or whether it contains nuclear materials. It is imperative that we carry out our verification activities in Iran without further delay.”

#131. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#126. Eisenkot condemns Netanyahu’s ‘racist’ campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens

Israeli prime ministerial hopeful Gadi Eisenkot condemned a “racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation” against the country’s Arab citizens, after the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, accused Arab parties of electoral fraud without evidence.

Netanyahu has repeatedly targeted Arab parties and Palestinian citizens of Israel after his party received a disappointing electoral showing, accusing them of manipulating the election and being linked to Hamas. Israeli electoral authorities have rejected these claims as not being backed by evidence.

Arab citizens of Israel, the vast majority of them of Palestinian descent, comprise more than 20% of the country’s population.

Shin Bet, Israel’s internal security branch, sought to question four Arab lawmakers, including the Arab-majority Ra’am party head, Mansour Abbas, leading to accusations that the security agency had become politicised.

“Arab citizens of Israel are an integral part of the Israeli story. The attempt to marginalise them and vilify them collectively is a profound violation of the principles of statesmanship,” Eisenkot said in a statement on Friday.

Eisenkot’s statement was a rare instance of a prominent Jewish political leader taking a strong stand in defence of Arab Israelis.

The statement also reflected political realities in Israel, as Arab parties found themselves kingmakers after the latest election. The two Arab-majority parties, Ra’am and the Joint List, secured 19 seats in the October election.

They have the potential to help Eisenkot form a governing coalition and become Israel’s next prime minister. If Eisenkot cannot form a coalition within 28 days, with a possible 14-day extension, the mandate to form a government would pass to another lawmaker, potentially Netanyahu.

Eisenkot has to balance the competing interests of several parties if he hopes to form a coalition. Rightwing elements of the opposition have pledged to drop their support of the prospective PM if he cooperates with Arab parties.

His statement on Friday portrayed a more statesman-like approach to ruling than his more rightwing, demagogic opponents who have used Arab parties and citizens as political scapegoats over the last three years.

“[Arab citizens] have been subjected to a racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation, fuelled by the outgoing prime minister and those around him for political purposes,” Eisenkot said in his statement.

“The prime minister of Israel is the prime minister of all Israeli citizens, including those who did not vote for him,” he added.

Netanyahu and his allies have repeatedly criticised leaders of Arab parties for refusing to describe Hamas as a “terror group” after its unprecedented 7 October 2023 attack on Israel.

All leaders of Arab parties have condemned the attack, in which Arab Israelis – descendants of Palestinians who remained in what became Israel in 1948 – were also among those killed.

In a speech, Netanyahu falsely claimed that the two Arab lists sought to establish either an “Islamic state” or a “nationalist Palestinian state”.

During the same ceremony, the national security minister, Itamar Ben Gvir, heckled Joint List’s leader, Yousef Jabareen, during his oath of allegiance, accusing him of having “pledged allegiance to Hamas”.

“It is entirely legitimate to disagree with representatives of the Arab parties, even to strongly disapprove of some of them. But it is neither legitimate nor acceptable to malign an entire community, and I will not allow it,” Eisenkot said.

President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with forming a new government on Sunday after consultations in which a majority of political parties, including the two Arab lists, recommended him for the role.

Israeli media has reported that Eisenkot has also been reaching out to the country’s ultra-Orthodox parties for a potential alliance. The issue of the ultra-Orthodox not serving in the Israeli military was one of the hot button issues during the October election, with Eisenkot previously saying they should also be included in the country’s mandatory draft.

#124. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#123. Germany's 2027 budget: A record €561 billion and a mountain of debt

The 2027 federal budget proposal has finally been hammered out and is set for a vote in parliament. What are the government's policy priorities in defense, infrastructure, the economy and social spending?

It was 5:55 a.m. on Friday when the Bundestag's Budget Committee concluded its final session.

Following nearly 16 hours of negotiations, lawmakers from the conservative Christian Democratic Union(CDU) and Christian Social Union (CSU) parties, along with their coalition partners from the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD), voted in favor of the 2027 draft budget. The opposition parties — the environmentalist Greens, the socialist Left Party and the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) — voted against it. With that, the way is now clear for the final vote in the Bundestag on November 27.

Reading through the nearly 1,000-page document requires a great deal of number-crunching. Next year, the federal government plans to spend around €561 billion ($620 billion).

But here's the problem: it won't collect nearly enough revenue to cover these expenses. With a shortfall of €123 billion, borrowing will be needed to bridge the gap.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

But that's not the only problem. On top of all this debt, Germany also has loans for defense spending and for the renovation of the dilapidated infrastructure. Approved by the Bundestag in 2025, these special funds are set to be in place for multiple years.

If the new debt for 2027 is factored in, the total borrowing amounts to over €210 billion. This is the second-highest level of borrowing in the history of the Federal Republic of Germany. The only time Germany borrowed more was during the COVID-19 pandemic in 2021, when borrowing reached €215.4 billion.

This development has drawn sharp criticism from opposition parties. Green Party lawmaker Sebastian Schäfer and his colleague Dietmar Bartsch of the Left Party called it a "gigantic mountain of debt," an "untrustworthy" budget, "irresponsible" policies at the expense of future generations and a lack of future prospects.

To understand the reasons behind the growing debt, one has to look beyond the numbers. Every federal budget is a snapshot of the priorities set by a government and a country — in terms of the economy, social issues, security and geopolitics.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For 2027, that story continues to be one of crises and wars. While Russia's war against Ukraine is driving up defense spending, the faltering economy is also demanding new investments. At the same time, Germany's population is aging rapidly, which is driving up social spending.

What's more, the crumbling infrastructure must be repaired and modernized, and the energy transition is costing billions. And more than half of all this debt is going toward security and defense.

Russia's war against Ukraine will enter its sixth year in 2027. As of the end of June, the federal government estimated its overall support for Ukraine to have reached approximately €100 billion. In response to a request from the AfD in the Bundestag, the government stated: "The federal government will continue to provide Ukraine with political, financial, humanitarian, civil and military support for as long as necessary."

The rearmament of the Bundeswehr and spending on NATO are also putting massive pressure on the budget. The defense budget is set to grow to just under €110 billion in 2027, an increase of around €27 billion compared to last year. Added to this are expenditures from the Bundeswehr's special fund.

Nevertheless, by far the largest single budget item comes from the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs, which is set to grow to over €200 billion. There are plans for cost-cutting measures, for example, in basic income support for job seekers. But the costs of old-age pension benefits continue to rise.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

People born in the 1960s have begun to retire. Back then, more than 1 million children were born each year. But birth rates nearly halved in the early 1970s and have continued to decline since then. As a result, there will be an increasing number of retirees and a decreasing number of working-age individuals in the coming years.

The pension funds cannot be financed by the pension contributions of this dwindling workforce and have to be subsidized by the government. Already, contributions to the pension insurance system — which are split equally between employees and employers — will have to be increased. They are expected to rise to just under 20% of gross salary.

The Federal Employment Agency, too, has long been unable to make ends meet on contributions alone. In 2027, subsidies of more than €8 billion from the federal budget are planned.

Anyone comparing Germany's current budget situation with that of past years will be rubbing their eyes in disbelief. It wasn't that long ago that Germany was considered a model of fiscal discipline. Between 2014 and 2019, the federal budget managed to avoid taking on new debt for six consecutive years. In some years, there was even money left over.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

But the era of "balanced budgets" came to an abrupt end with the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020. However, today Germany is still enjoying the benefits of those years of fiscal stability. Compared to other countries, Germany is still in relatively good shape.

The key indicator is not the amount of debt along but the debt-to-GDP ratio. It indicates how high the national debt is in relation to annual economic output.

At the end of 2025, it stood at 63.5% for Germany. France, on the other hand, stands at over 110% and Italy at about 135% to 140%, while the US is well above 100%. However, in its latest fall report, the German Council of Economic Experts warned that Germany could be heading down a dangerous path. If debt continues to rise at this rate, the debt-to-GDP ratio could climb to 100% by 2040.

This would also put Germany's creditworthiness on the financial markets at risk. Germany is the only country among the seven leading democratic industrialized nations (G7) that still holds a triple-A (AAA) rating. This is the highest possible credit rating on the financial markets. It signals to investors that the risk of default on German government bonds is extremely low.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

This also means that interest rates on new loans are not set as high. This is an important point, as interest payments place an increasing burden on the federal budget each year. By 2027, approximately €42 billion in interest payments are expected to be due.

At the end of November, the Bundestag will hold its final debate on the draft budget. Should changes be expected by then? There is disagreement within the government over whether a reduction in the electricity tax might still be possible.

The prospect of such a reduction had been raised by Chancellor Friedrich Merz. On Friday, during a European leaders summit in Dublin, he reiterated: "I expect that we will discuss this again next Tuesday in the coalition committee, and I remain committed to my goal of lowering the electricity tax to the European minimum — in full — starting next year."

#120. Russia is ‘setting traps’ in Ukraine talks, EU foreign policy chief warns

Kaja Kallas says Moscow is trying to divert attention from its aggression by raising issue of European security

The EU’s foreign policy chief has warned against “walking into the traps that Russia is setting” as she said Moscow was trying to start a debate about European security in order to divert attention from its aggression.

“We have one aggressor and one victim, and the aggressor does not have security concerns,” Kaja Kallas said on arrival at an EU leaders’ summit in Dublin. “Nobody has ever attacked them. They have attacked others. I mean, keep the focus, people.”

In talks on the Ukraine war, Russia has revived its hardline demands for a total overhaul of European security – demands that were rejected in 2021 because they were seen as an infringement of the sovereignty of central and eastern European countries. The latest round of talks between Ukraine, Russia and the US was held this week in Abu Dhabi.

Donald Trump’s envoy and son-in-law, Jared Kushner, said on Thursday night that Russia and Ukraine had agreed to expand the next round of peace negotiations to include the Europeans. However, Russia quickly contradicted that assurance.

European leaders have long said they will support Ukraine in any peace talks, but want to see a ceasefire before negotiations. They also stress the continent has an extensive web of security agreements that Russia has violated.

Friedrich Merz, the German chancellor, said: “Russia wants to talk about questions of a European security architecture. We do not need to discuss that; it already exists.” He added that it was a “given” that his country would be part of any European delegation.

The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, said it was “self-explanatory that Europe is included in the peace talks to safeguard our interests, but also to support Ukraine”.

The Kremlin said on Friday: “Once we understand [Europe’s] real intentions and their willingness to contribute to the peace process, then we can make a decision on holding talks in an expanded format.” Russia’s lead negotiator in Abu Dhabi, Vladimir Medinsky, said it was “unclear what there is to discuss” with Europe.

The US call to involve Europe in talks has turned the spotlight on who would represent it.

Speaking after a summit of EU leaders in Dublin on Friday, the European Council president, António Costa, said leaders had agreed on a negotiating team: the EU institutions and E5 countries, namely Britain, France, Germany, Italy and Poland.

“Europe must speak with one clear and united voice in all discussions that shape the future of Ukraine and the security of our continent,” he said. “This is ultimately a team effort.”

The format had already emerged in recent months, but the need to formalise the grouping gained momentum after Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, this week urged his European allies to decide who will represent Europe in negotiations.

Kushner also mocked the absence of a single person to speak for the entire continent. “If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiations,” he said earlier this week.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

But there was an increasingly settled view that Europe would not be represented by a single person, but by different interlocutors from the wider continent. Paris and Berlin had argued for a continued role for their own envoys in the E3 format of Britain, France and Germany, alongside EU representatives. Smaller member states put the emphasis more firmly on the EU representatives: Costa and Kallas, the EU’s high representative for foreign affairs and security policy.

Earlier in the day, Sweden’s prime minister, Ulf Kristersson, said the discussion on who should represent Europe was “slightly overstated” as it had differing interests.

Some of the issues in peace talks were “pure EU interests”, he said, citing financing Kyiv and Ukraine’s future membership in the bloc. He also supported other formats, such as the “pretty uncomplicated” E3 and the “very legitimate” E5, which includes Italy and Poland, as well as a role for EU representatives. He said: “I am quite flexible on that. I think what we actually say is more important than the exact configuration of the people in the room.”

Several at the summit, including Kallas, also pointed to the importance of including the UK, a founder member of the coalition of the willing, a broad group of countries committed to future peacekeeping in Ukraine.

Ireland’s taoiseach, Micheál Martin, said the UK and Norway each had a significant role and “that all has to be taken into consideration because it’s a European, continent-wide issue”.

The British prime minister, Andy Burnham, said it was for the EU to decide who would represent the bloc. He said there was a strong view among European leaders that it was for Europe – in the wider sense – “to decide its own arrangements” on security.

#117. SpaceX carries out first-ever spaceship capture

The successful recovery of SpaceX's Starship marks a breakthrough in founder Elon Musk’s goal of developing a fully reusable rocket system.

SpaceX's Starship spacecraft returned from its longest-yet flight in orbit on Saturday to the tower it was launched from on Friday.

The spaceship descended and smoothly slipped between the mechanical "chopstick arms" above Launch Pad 2 in the US state of Texas. It was the first key recovery of the rocket system's centerpiece vessel in the company's decadelong effort to develop a fully reusable rocket.

"Can you believe it?" SpaceX commentator Dan Huot shouted. "Look at her! Baby, she’s back!"

It was the 15th test flight of the world's biggest and most powerful rocket system. The Starship spacecraft orbited Earth 14 times for over 22 hours, during which it deployed 26 Starlink satellites.

The successful return marked a ​key milestone in the multibillion-dollar development of Starship, which billionaire Elon Musk sees ​as crucial to SpaceX's satellite business and ambitions to put humans on the Moon and eventually Mars.

NASA welcomed the news. The US space agency is paying billions of dollars for Starship to land Artemis astronauts on the moon later this decade and help build a permanent lunar base.

"It was almost magic," NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman wrote on X.

SpaceX also intended to capture the bottom part of the rocket, known as the Super Heavy booster, but the lower stage recovery was aborted, and it exploded over the Gulf of Mexico shortly after launch on Friday.

SpaceX's Kate Tice said its engines stopped firing too soon. The booster's guidance system sensed it was not in the correct position and initiated the self-destruction protocol. Engineers are still investigating the cause.

SpaceX caught the Super Heavy Booster on three earlier test flights. Developing a fully reusable rocket system is key to SpaceX’s goal to drive down the cost of spaceflight.

#116. Hungary seizes $19.5m in cash found buried in forests amid graft inquiry

Anti-corruption agency carried out searches as part of investigation into Orbán-era minister accused of taking bribes

Hungary’s anti-corruption agency said on Friday it had seized cash worth $19.5m (£14.8m) buried in forests and hidden elsewhere while investigating an ex-minister of nationalist former leader Viktor Orbán’s cabinet.

Miklós Seszták, Hungary’s minister of national development between 2014 and 2018, is accused of taking bribes in a scheme involving the state transport company, purchasing secondhand buses at inflated prices.

The prime minister, Péter Magyar, won a landslide election victory in April, ending Orbán’s 16 years in power after promising to investigate alleged corruption and abuse of office during his rival’s rule.

Seszták, two other Orbán-era ministers and the former premier’s brother have been taken into custody in different cases as graft inquiries have gathered pace in recent weeks.

The National Asset Recovery and Protection Office (NVVH), an anti-graft body with sweeping powers established as part of Magyar’s accountability drive, said it searched several locations on Thursday and Friday as part of the criminal proceedings against Seszták.

“During the search, investigators found large amounts of cash: buried in forests, hidden in agricultural and warehouse buildings as well as residential properties, and stored in high-value safes in secluded locations,” the statement said.

“The total amount of cash found during the two-day search was 5,071,632,000 forints, €2,658,350 and US $1,257,100,” it added, bringing the combined value to $19.5m.

NVVH said its staff deposited the cash into the Hungarian State Treasury’s account.

“Upon receipt, 12 Treasury employees spent 8 hours counting the money using 5 counting machines,” the finance minister, András Kármán, said on Facebook.

Magyar hailed the seizure as a potential turning point.

“This officially marks the end of the Fidesz-KDNP mafia!” he said in reference to Orbán’s former governing coalition.

The former leader did not react to the latest development. Orbán had previously denounced the probes targeting his inner circle as “political persecution” aimed at imprisoning him.

#110. University hounded out of Hungary by Orbán reaches out to new government

Prospect of partial return to Budapest by Central European University suggests confidence in Hungary’s future

A university that Viktor Orbán tried to chase out of Hungary is open to expanding its presence in Budapest, in a mark of confidence in the country’s democratic future.

Carsten Q Schneider, the interim president and rector of Central European University (CEU), said it was interested in increasing its programmes in Budapest, as long as this did not damage the student experience.

So far the CEU has not held formal talks with Hungary’s new government, which has promised to restore academic freedom, “but our hand is reaching out, their hand is reaching out – it is just that they have many things to do”, the academic told the Guardian.

The CEU relocated its main activities to Vienna from Budapest in 2019, after pressure from the previous government led by Orbán. But the outlook changed when Péter Magyar won a landslide victory in April pledging to root out corruption and reform the state.

The CEU, founded by Hungarian-American financier and philanthropist George Soros in 1991 to entrench democratic change in the region, was forced to cease awarding degrees in Budapest in response to a law widely seen as targeting the institution.

The EU’s highest court struck down Hungary’s “Lex CEU” in 2020, finding that it violated the EU charter of fundamental rights, but the verdict came too late for the university. The CEU retains 312 staff in Budapest, mostly administrative, but the majority, 476, and all its teaching, are in Vienna.

Schneider said upgrading the status of the CEU, which is privately funded, in Budapest would be “a symbol for academic freedom [and] of resilience” but depended on goodwill and funding independent of the Hungarian government. He said a decision on its future in Hungary would be taken by the CEU’s 13-member board, whose members include Soros and his son, Alexander Soros.

“We don’t want special treatment,” Schneider said. ‘“We are a great asset, so let’s work out something.”

But the CEU, which he said was “thriving tremendously” in Vienna, does not plan a wholesale return to Budapest. Any change would have to ensure the quality of the student experience, for instance, assuring that students would be able to combine courses, say in economics and history, without having to travel the 200km-plus distance between the two cities.

Focused on social sciences, law and humanities, the CEU has more than 1,400 students from 103 countries, mostly doing master’s and PhD programmes.

According to Schneider, for the first time in the CEU’s 25-year history, students from the US now make up the largest cohort of master’s students (10%). The CEU has seen an increase in US applicants since Donald Trump returned to the White House and launched a sweeping campaign against elite universities, including slashing federal funding and seeking to exert control over internal policies.

The CEU rector has had informal talks with US academics on how to maintain academic freedom under pressure from rightwing populists. He described his message as: “Don’t be naive for a second. These people have a plan, so it’s not by accident that they attack universities. They want to have cultural hegemony.” He added, however, that Trump and allied movements, such as Germany’s Alternative für Deutschland, have identified “real problems”, such as in the US how universities have entrenched “a reproduction of elites”

When coming under political pressure, he said universities needed “to look for allies”, among other academic institutions, students, alumni and parents. Institutions also had to stand up for disciplines under attack, such as gender studies, Schneider added.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

He recalled the outpouring of support for CEU – when tens of thousands of people protested in Budapest in defence of the university – as “one of the most humbling experiences I have had”.

Schneider, a German political scientist who specialises in the study of democracies and regime transition, said Magyar had successfully used Orbán’s political machine against him.

“Nobody has a better idea than [Magyar] how to defeat the most potent and the most skilled illiberal populist [Orbán]. So he knows something.” He added: “The people and skills that you need to bring a non-democratic regime down are not the same and sometimes the opposite of what it takes to establish a democracy. So the question now is whether he [Magyar] has the skills to basically reinvent liberal democracy for the 21st century.”

Schneider said Hungary could be the world’s first “post-illiberal democracy” – a model for democratic restoration, just as Orbán inspired rightwing populists around the world. But he also raised doubts about this thesis, saying there were questions about how Magyar and his Tisza party might develop in the context of Hungary’s “very difficult economic situation”.

The test would be, he suggested, when Magyar faces losing support and, eventually, defeat at the ballot box. “So what happens when he loses support and then cracks in his movement appear? Will he be the visionary leader who manages … to establish a three, four, five-party system, where people compete? That is to be seen.”

#109. Here’s the latest.

The stoppage stranded thousands of passengers at airports across the country. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said old equipment was overloaded, causing “instability” in radar communications.

The nationwide ground stop that brought all air travel to a halt across the country and stranded thousands of passengers has been canceled, according to the Federal Aviation Administration, though delays from the outage are expected to persist in the coming days.

The order on Friday halted nearly all departures and stranded thousands of passengers at airports across the country. Hundreds of flights were diverted to other airports and more were canceled, even as some internationally bound flights managed to secure permission to take off. Many passengers were taken off flights or told to go home. Chaos spread at airports as travelers sought answers from airline and airport employees.

Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, warned that it would take “some time” for air travel nationwide to recover from the ground stop. Speaking at a news conference on Friday night shortly after flight traffic had been restored, Mr. Duffy predicted that flight schedules would be fully back to normal by the time the Thanksgiving travel season begins.

“I believe that it’s going to take us some time to get back to capacity,” he said, adding: “We’re going to have some makeup to do.”

Mr. Duffy decried Congress, accusing lawmakers of dragging their feet when it came to fully funding upgrades he warned were vital to prevent similar failures from happening again.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Mr. Duffy told reporters, adding: “How many times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

Mr. Duffy said that the antiquated radar and telecoms systems that controllers rely on to transmit data and other information between facilities began to falter at 3:36 p.m. The aging system, which has undergone a number of upgrades, became overloaded, officials said.

Appearing alongside him, Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, stressed that there was never any indication that the communications failures had been triggered by a cyberattack, but he added, “we are still looking, and we’re not going to rule it out.” He emphasized that lives were never in danger.

But the incident appeared to inject new fuel into Mr. Duffy’s now monthslong campaign to secure at least $30 billion in extra funding for air traffic control modernization.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to upgrade aging air traffic facilities and technology, funds Mr. Duffy has repeatedly referred to as a “down payment” when asking for tens of billions more. On Friday night, Mr. Duffy appeared to up the ante, telling reporters that he didn’t just want another $30 billion for the project, but another $50 billion. He argued that it should be delivered in emergency funding before the end of the year, after which control of Congress — and the budget — switches from Republicans to Democrats.

Some Democratic lawmakers have already raised questions about how efficiently and effectively the F.A.A. has spent the money they have been given thus far. But Mr. Duffy has long argued that his department cannot be expected to complete a meaningful overhaul of the nation’s air traffic control system without a massive influx of funding.

Friday’s nationwide ground stop was only the third that the F.A.A. has issued this century. It ranks as the second-longest, after the ground stop issued on Sept. 11, 2001, in the wake of the terrorist attacks on the World Trade Center in New York. There was a shorter one on Jan. 11, 2023, because of an outage in the F.A.A.’s system to alert pilots to safety issues.

More than 2 million people pass through airport security checkpoints on a typical day, according to Transportation Security Administration data. On Thursday, the agency screened nearly 2.5 million people. According to FlightRadar24, an aviation tracking site, there were more than 1,000 airborne aircraft bound for U.S. airports during the nationwide ground stop. According to the flight tracking app Flighty, there were more than 30,000 departures and arrivals scheduled for the remainder of the day.

It could take days for air travel to fully normalize after an event of this scale, said Vikrant Vaze, a Dartmouth professor specializing in commercial aviation logistics.

“It’s going to take a lot of gymnastics” to get every airline’s aircraft, passengers and crew members back in the correct positions, he said.

If your flight is canceled: You are entitled to a refund if your flight is canceled in the United States, regardless of the reason, or if a flight is significantly delayed or changed and you decide not to take it. Here are other travel tips that might be helpful. Read more ›

A busy travel month: The ground stop happened about two weeks before Thanksgiving, one of the busiest times of the year for air travel. On average, U.S. airlines are expected to fly about 2.7 million passengers each day over the holiday period, according to industry trade group Airlines for America.

Handling the chaos: Radio transmissions between air traffic controllers and pilots at major airports offered a peek at how they navigated the ground stoppage. Read more ›

The last ground stop: The last nationwide ground stop came in the early morning of Jan. 11, 2023, and lasted around 90 minutes. The disruption came after a system to alert pilots of real-time dangers went down. Read more ›

Christine Chung, Niraj Chokshi, Luke Broadwater, Dustin Volz, Lisa Lerer and Robert Chiarito contributed reporting.

At Los Angeles International Airport, a number of travelers who had been hoping to get a late flight out have now been notified that their flights have been canceled. Many travelers here are scrambling to find new flights for Saturday as well as accommodation for the night.

Their flight to Jacksonville cancelled, longtime friends Ann Bruns, 79, and Janice Simpson, 78, opted to hire a driver to shuttle them six hours from Atlanta to Florida, where they hope to catch a cruise ship departing on Sunday.

“We’re about to go on a cruise ship with no clothes,” Ms. Simpson explained, because their luggage had already been loaded onto a plane bound for Jacksonville.

Ms. Bruns described a day punctuated by confusion, saying when the duo arrived in Atlanta from Kansas City, “We saw pilots just standing there, and we knew something was up.” They still feel like they have almost no idea what went wrong to send air travel into turmoil.

The Federal Aviation Administration issued a rare nationwide ground stop Friday afternoon, after outdated air traffic communications equipment became overloaded with data and began to fail, officials said.

The outage stranded thousands of passengers and caused widespread delays from which airlines and airports are still struggling to recover. It also highlighted persistent vulnerabilities in the aging U.S. air traffic infrastructure and a funding fight, as administration officials demanded lawmakers approve tens of billions of dollars more to accelerate systemwide upgrades.

Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, told reporters at the White House Friday night that the radar and other air traffic communications started to falter at 3:36 p.m. when old routers became overloaded with data. While the failures were not universal, they were pervasive enough to prompt officials to issue the unusual nationwide moratorium on takeoffs, Mr. Duffy said.

Appearing alongside him at the White House, Markwayne Mullin, the homeland security secretary, stressed that there was no evidence that nefarious activity had triggered the technology failures.

“We’ve seen zero evidence that this was a cyberattack,” Mr. Mullin said, suggesting that new equipment and new technology that Mr. Duffy’s department had added to the old infrastructure was what had overwhelmed it.

Mr. Duffy insisted that the failure was caused by the antiquated, legacy communications equipment, not recent upgrades to the system.

“What we have is an incredibly old air traffic control system,” Mr. Duffy said, predicting that until significant upgrades are made, “it’s going to continue to fail.”

Mr. Duffy warned that it would take “some time” for air travel nationwide to recover from the ground stop, predicting flight schedules would be fully back to normal by the time the Thanksgiving travel season begins.

“I believe that it’s going to take us some time to get back to capacity,” he said, adding: “We’re going to have some makeup to do.”

Mr. Duffy said that the F.A.A. would prioritize larger commercial flights ahead of private and charter flights, to get previously grounded flights off the ground.

Mr. Duffy has long been pushing for a sweeping overhaul of the U.S. air traffic infrastructure, routinely complaining that controllers have been hampered by the country’s failure to upgrade outdated equipment.

“We have old, old switches, old routers — we use 1980s little copper lines,” he said to reporters Friday. Only last week, the F.A.A. announced that it had begun to phase out the use of floppy disks.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to fund upgrades to the country’s aging air traffic facilities and networks, some of which has been directed toward communications system upgrades. Mr. Duffy noted Friday that air traffic facilities that had been upgraded to digital systems did not experience the same overloading issues that prompted the F.A.A. to issue the ground stop order.

But he stressed that having an air traffic control system dependent on a “patchwork” of upgrades and fixes was untenable, calling on Congress to spend tens of billions more to allow for more upgrades.

For months, Mr. Duffy has been calling on the Republican-controlled Congress to give his department another $30 billion to accelerate efforts to upgrade air traffic control systems. On Friday night, he upped the ante, telling reporters he wanted $50 billion — and that he wanted it before the end of the year.

“This is an emergency, I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” he said, adding: “How many more times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

But it is unclear whether Republicans will prioritize such a sum before the end of the year, after which control of Congress shifts to the Democrats, who are expected to scrutinize how the F.A.A. has utilized air traffic funding they have received so far. Republican lawmakers have filed bills in the House and Senate to authorize $30 billion in air traffic upgrades, but neither piece of legislation has cosponsors.

Later on Friday, Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the House’s top-ranking Democrat, appeared to reject Mr. Duffy’s accusing tone and shifted the blame back to the Trump administration.

“Today’s unprecedented breakdown is the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump administration’s failed leadership,” he said in a statement, adding that “American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump administration about how and why this happened and assurances that steps are being taken to ensure that our air travel system remains safe and functional.”

Nationwide, more than 8,200 flights — or over 31 percent of those scheduled in the United States for Friday — were canceled, according to Cirium, an aviation data firm. And the disruption was already spilling over into Saturday, usually a slower day for travel, with more than 5 percent of flights canceled before the day had even begun.

Reporting from Denver, where they are stranded along with much of the Democratic Party.

Pete Buttigieg could not believe what he had just watched. The former transportation secretary stood at the corner of the hotel bar in Denver, where CNN had just finished broadcasting the Trump administration’s news conference about the ground stop that stopped flights nationwide on Friday.

Mr. Buttigieg was among the thousands of Democrats who had gathered in Denver this week for a conference designed to chart the party’s future and celebrate their midterm wins. The ground stop stranded some of the party’s top rising stars and their staffs.

People accustomed to having inside information suddenly had very little.

At a table to Mr. Buttigieg’s left sat Gov. Abigail Spanberger of Virginia, Gov. Mikie Sherrill of New Jersey and Senator Elissa Slotkin of Michigan. They munched on Parmesan fries and checked their phones as they waited for news of their flights.

To his right, Senator Raphael Warnock of Georgia scanned his Senate group chats to check for updates from secure channels and Senator Mark Warner of Virginia.

But Mr. Buttigieg, glancing up at the television, struggled to comprehend the performance of his successor, Secretary Sean Duffy.

“You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Mr. Buttigieg said when a New York Times reporter asked for his impressions of the news conference. “I have questions.”

The downtown hotel bar resembled the famous bar scene in “Star Wars.” The stranded politicians bemoaned the lack of information from the federal government and their inability to get home.

One New England-based operative booked a rental car and planned to drive back to Boston. Others looked up how many hours it would take to get to the East Coast on Amtrak. (The answer: more than 40.) Aides to one prominent Democratic governor, who had flown to Denver on a private jet, fielded questions from others in the bar about whether they could hitch a ride home.

A few people working for Gov. JB Pritzker of Illinois — who did not come to Denver — commandeered a long table where Anne Caprara, his chief of staff, worked two phones as she juggled rumors, emergencies back home and calls from the boss. She said Mr. Pritzker was furious about the lack of information coming from the Trump administration.

“Welcome to Denver’s hottest bar, the Mos Eisley Cantina of politics,” Ms. Caprara said, referring to the “Star Wars” bar. “With ‘Veep’ characters and a ‘Terminator 2’ vibe.”

Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the leader of the House Democrats, shifted the blame for Friday’s ground stop back to the Trump administration. In a statement, he called the stop “the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump Administration’s failed leadership.”

Earlier Friday night, Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, had blamed Congress for not adequately funding his department’s plans to upgrade the nation’s air traffic infrastructure.

“American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump Administration about how and why this happened,” Mr. Jeffries added.

A communications outage on Friday led the Federal Aviation Administration to pause departing flights nationwide, but pilots were not entirely cut off from federal air traffic controllers.

In a Friday night news conference, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said the outage was intermittent, so federal controllers were still able to reach pilots.

“It wasn’t a complete blackout in communication or radar,” he said. “So we were able to keep some of those communications going.”

But even in a complete outage, aviation authorities have multiple backups to maintain contact with pilots.

Pilots and air traffic controllers primarily communicate by voice radio, using primary, backup and emergency frequencies.

Controllers and airline dispatchers can also send text-based messages to pilots. And many commercial planes are equipped with satellite communications, which are especially useful for reaching people on the ground when the planes are flying in remote airspace like over the ocean.

If a control tower loses radio capabilities, it can also communicate with pilots with high-intensity colored lights, which pilots can acknowledge by rocking their wings or flashing lights in response.

Airlines began to restore some flights after the nationwide stop was lifted. United Airlines said that while many of its flights had been canceled, some were still expected to operate.

American Airlines said it was prioritizing flights to non-hub airports and international destinations in order to get planes and crews in place to recover quickly. Southwest Airlines said it was resuming a “limited number of flights” on Friday.

Some professional and college sports teams were stuck on runways, or opting to pile into buses, ahead of games this weekend that are now uncertain due to the nationwide ground stop that was lifted Friday night.

Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, said at a news conference on Friday that the ground stop was caused by technology upgrades “overwhelming” the flight communication system. Thousands of flights have been canceled, but no high-profile games have been affected so far, ahead of what is set to be a busy weekend in sports.

Dozens of teams had made it out of airports just ahead of the stop. But the N.B.A.’s Brooklyn Nets were not so lucky.

The Athletic reported that the Nets were stuck on a team plane on Friday. They have a game Saturday night against the Memphis Grizzles.

Also stuck at an airport was the University of Texas Longhorns football team, slated to play Louisiana State University in Baton Rouge, La., on Saturday night. Minutes before the stoppage, the Longhorns posted a video of players boarding a plane with the caption “Bayou bound.”

Both teams are in the Southeastern Conference, which said in a statement that it would work “to determine whether any adjustments to scheduled competitions become necessary.”

On Friday night, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy told reporters that he encouraged college football teams to coordinate their flights with the Federal Aviation Administration “to make sure we have games going off tomorrow.”

While some teams, including the Longhorns, headed home from the airport to wait out the delay, a handful of others opted to travel the old-fashioned way: By bus.

The Athletic reported that among the longest drives was a 330-mile ride for the Purdue University Boilermakers, who are playing the University of Iowa Hawkeyes on Saturday night.

The University of Southern California women’s basketball team said they were grounded in Los Angeles ahead of a marquee game Sunday against the University of South Carolina Gamecocks, but were preparing to travel on Saturday.

In men’s college basketball, a Saturday game between University of North Carolina Greensboro and Cornell University in Ithaca, N.Y., was postponed because of the ground stop.

N.F.L. teams typically travel on Saturday for Sunday games. Five teams told The Athletic that their travel plans were not affected.

According to The Athletic, the stakes of the ground stop may be most significant for the National Women’s Soccer League, which is scheduled to hold its semifinals this weekend. The stop affected the Kansas City Current’s flight to New York City ahead of its game against Gotham FC on Sunday. The Utah Royals, playing the Washington Spirit on Saturday, traveled before the stoppage.

The N.B.A.’s Cleveland Cavaliers had planned to catch a flight home after their Friday night game in Miami. Kenny Atkinson, the Cavaliers head coach, told reporters before the game that he didn’t know if the team would make their flight.

“I think we’re scrambling to find hotel rooms in case,” Mr. Atkinson said, adding: “If not, we’ll sleep on the bus.”

Alexander CardiaNeil Bedi and Robin Stein

Radio transmissions between active pilots and air traffic controllers at major airports offer a peek at how they navigated the Federal Aviation Administration’s nationwide ground stoppage on Friday.

An air traffic controller at Newark Liberty International Airport can be heard sharing the news with pilots.

Minutes later, at airports in Atlanta and Chicago, controllers said they had limited information.

As planes landed over the next hour and departures were stuck at their gates, air traffic controllers can be heard talking about significant gate issues and comparing the tarmac to a “parking lot.”

Amid the chaos, one pilot noted the date while talking to a Newark controller: “It’s not lost on me that it’s Friday the 13th.”

Passengers groaned loudly as an announcement at Newark Liberty International Airport ordered them back to their gates for both domestic and international flights. “Are you kidding me? When will this end?” one woman cried out.

At Kennedy International Airport in New York, passengers waited to board a flight to Paris that was delayed because the pilot hadn’t been able to take his flight from Boston to J.F.K. Instead, he decided to drive, and passengers have been receiving updates about his E.T.A. over the loudspeaker. The latest update said he was expected to arrive at the airport at 9:45 p.m. When it was announced that the flight would begin boarding before his arrival, one woman raised both hands over her head with her fingers crossed.

More than 6,600 flights on Friday had been canceled by 9:30 p.m. Eastern, according to Flight Aware, a flight-tracking service. And that disruption had already spilled over into the weekend, with about 1,500 flights on Saturday canceled as well.

Pete Buttigieg, who served as the transportation secretary during the Biden administration, is stranded in Denver and mingling in the bar at the Ritz-Carlton hotel. After watching the Trump administration’s press conference, Buttigieg seemed exasperated. “You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Buttigieg said. “I have questions.”

It could take days for air travel to fully normalize after an event of this scale, said Vikrant Vaze, a Dartmouth professor specializing in commercial aviation logistics. “It’s going to take a lot of gymnastics” to get every airline’s aircraft, passengers and crew members back in the correct positions, he said.

Some contingency measures can be taken by airlines to get things back on track, such as flying reserve crews or “ferrying” an empty aircraft. It’s helpful that airlines have the nighttime hours, when there are typically fewer domestic flights, to push through some of the backlog, Vaze said.

Cheers and applause erupted in the Delta Sky Club at Los Angeles International Airport, where travelers just heard an announcement that the nationwide ground stop had been lifted. At least three Delta flights at the airport were already boarding, but it could take some time for other flights to get going, a Delta agent said in the announcement.

The White House briefing by the cabinet secretaries is now over.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy appeared to suggest that his effort to upgrade air traffic control infrastructure may have contributed to the outage, but he defended the upgrades by arguing that they had to be done.

“By doing nothing, you inject way more risk into the system,” he said, adding, “I didn’t anticipate that we would have an outage like this.”

Duffy has criticized Congress throughout this briefing for not allocating more funding to air traffic infrastructure.

“I can’t build things if I don’t have money,” Duffy said, shaking his head. “We should talk to our senators and members of Congress to say, ‘We don’t deserve this.’”

Sean Duffy has been clamoring for another $30 billion in air traffic modernization funds from Congress for several months now. On Friday night, he appeared to up the ante, saying he didn’t want just $30 billion — he wanted $50 billion. But the official ask, which he has made repeatedly and which is reflected in legislation he has endorsed, is another $30 billion.

In the last few weeks, Republicans in the House and Senate filed bills to give Duffy that $30 billion. Neither has any co-sponsors.

Asked if travelers should expect disruptions to their Thanksgiving travel plans, Sean Duffy said he thought flights would be “back up and operational as we come into the heat of travel” ahead of the holiday.

In the aftermath of a frightening air-traffic control technology failure that affected Newark Liberty International Airport in April 2025, Duffy argued that the nation’s air-traffic control systems were outdated and in need of a $12.5 billion upgrade. Tonight he is using the latest technology failure as an argument for another huge increase of funding.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said that new technology upgrades overloaded the existing, antiquated system, “overwhelming” the existing equipment.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said of the antiquated technology that the F.A.A. was trying to upgrade, triggering the telecom failures that prompted the nationwide ground stop.

The question of older F.A.A. systems ceasing to work has been a constant throughout Duffy’s tenure as transportation secretary, going back to disputes he had with Elon Musk about what type of technology could be used for backup systems.

The Congress that Duffy has been criticizing has been entirely in Republican hands for the last two years. It’s now about to switch to Democratic control.

“Our fix gives us a pretty good indication that it was a legacy network problem that went down,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, hinting at the origins of the crisis. He said the problem was related to old equipment being overloaded.

Duffy and Mullin were asked if they could say with “100 percent certainty” that this was not the result of a cyberattack or rogue artificial intelligence. “There’s nothing certain right now,” Mullin said. “It doesn’t appear that way.”

“I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, calling on lawmakers to give the Trump administration another $30 billion, at least, to pay for further upgrades to air traffic control infrastructure. But some lawmakers have raised questions about how the F.A.A. has managed the funds it has been given thus far.

#108. UK-EU breakthrough will allow airport-style checks at Eurotunnel to avoid border ‘chaos’

Eurotunnel owner Getlink wants to replicate ‘airport experience’ at Folkestone and Calais, without making people get out of cars

Airport-style passport and fingerprinting controls are to be introduced at Eurotunnel in an attempt to prevent “short-term and long-term chaos” in Folkestone and Calais.

In a breakthrough for travellers, the European Commission has agreed to a new system in which an “eagle eye” camera system, already used in Rome and Paris airports to supervise fingerprinting and facial imaging, will be used at the border between the UK and France.

Yann Leriche, the chief executive of Getlink, the Eurotunnel owner, said it had tried to replicate the “airport experience” but wanted to go much further.

Instead of getting out of the car and getting biometrics taken at a terminal building, holidaymakers and business travellers will now be greeted with special drive-through terminals in Folkestone and Calais.

Both sides are now equipped with 120 kiosks under the case of an eagle-eye camera that will be monitored by police, similar to e-gates at airports where police are not physically present at each gate.

In the coming weeks, Leriche will meet UK and French authorities presenting a 40-page proposal to allow Eurotunnel to become a “laboratory” for an “intelligent border” using emerging tech and lessons learned from the US and Singapore.

The entry and exit system (EES) requires all third-country citizens including British passport holders to submit fingerprints and facial images as they enter and exit the Schengen area, but has been delayed for years.

EES was supposed to be active across the EU by 9 September, but nine of the 27 EU member states, including France, have yet to be deploy it fully because of continued technical issues.

It has always been a logistical nightmare for Eurotunnel and a potential cause of miles-long tailbacks at the approach to the terminal.

Originally, Getlink had planned to use a tablet to hand to the passengers in their cars to allow them to self-fingerprint and capture a facial image. It also looked at a system involving passengers parking up and going into the terminal to get fingerprinting.

But both models would have required the physical presence of police officers for first-time fingerprinting.

Leriche said: “We told the authorities: it is going to be a mess. Many people are going to the wrong place and you can’t have police jumping from one place to the next to solve the issues. It would also means customers would have a bad experience, particularly at peak time.

“This is not something people in airports experience, but nobody approached this before, thinking we could have an airport experience at the border here. But we said: why not?”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He said that after “many, many discussions” with the European Commission and the French authorities, finally they were “authorised” to trial “eagle-eye cameras” at each kiosk. “The French authorities are also on board because for them it is more efficient, too, so we are all aligned on the trial,” he said.

The trial will last two years, but Eurotunnel hopes the experiment will become permanent.

Each car will be able to pull up to an individual kiosk, with two stands per vehicle to read passports and take fingerprints and facial images. “It will prevent the chaos in the long term and the short term and if the experiment works, as hopefully it will, then it will become permanent,” said Leriche.

He said the company had been inspired by visits to the US/Mexico border controls, where drivers and passengers remain in their cars.

The requirement of having a police officer present each time a first-time passenger, or “primo” as they are known, submits their fingerprints was designed as a safeguard against subterfuge. Criminals, for example, could use a fellow passenger for example with a “clean skin” fingerprint.

Leriche said he had no idea when the system will be introduced by France. “It has been delayed and delayed and delayed, but our kiosks are there, they can function but the connection with the French system is still not working. It is a question we are asking all the time.”

This week it emerged that the EU had filed a lawsuit against IBM and two other companies involved in the implementation of EES.

#107. Tens of thousands continue to protest across France

Some 106,000 people marched through French cities for a fifth consecutive weekend of protests, marking the lowest turnout in recent weeks.

Tens of thousands of protesters took to the streets across France on Saturday to demonstrate against the rising cost of living and strained public services, the Interior Ministry said.

Saturday's demonstrations drew their lowest turnout in recent weeks. Some 106,000 people marched through French cities, with 24,000 in Paris alone. The previous weekend, some 146,000 people had participated nationwide.

The number of arrests was also significantly lower. Police detained 219 people across the country, according to the Interior Ministry.

Although the demonstrations were largely peaceful, police fired tear gas in Paris, Bordeaux and the western city of Nantes, including after some protesters shot fireworks.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Mass protests have swept across the European Union's second-largest economy for more than a month.

What began in September as student protests against teacher shortages and poor facilities has since evolved into a broader movement fuelled by anger over the rising cost of living, strained public services and police brutality.

In mid-October, around 565,000 people took to the streets across France, as riots, looting and clashes disrupted demonstrations in several cities.

As France entered its fifth consecutive weekend of nationwide unrest, President Emmanuel Macron's approval ratings fell to the lowest level since he took office in 2017, according to recent polls.

The French government doesn't have much room to respond to the protesters' demands due to its high debt.

#106. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.
