#92. Thunderstorms, giant hail and powerful winds forecast for NSW as wild weather hits east coast

Volatile weather predicted after storms on Saturday, with a man in his 20s struck by lightning while swimming on NSW Central Coast

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Dangerous and severe “supercell” thunderstorms with the potential for giant hail and destructive winds were forecast for parts of New South Wales on Sunday, as volatile weather continued in the eastern states over the weekend.

Storms dumped heavy rain across large parts of northern and eastern NSW and much of Queensland on Saturday, with strong winds recorded up to 98km/h in Goulburn and rainfall totals of between 30mm and 50mm in the Coffs Harbour area.

A man in his 20s was struck by lightning while swimming at Ettalong beach on NSW’s Central Coast on Saturday afternoon. He was pulled from the water by fellow swimmers and given CPR by first responders until NSW Ambulance paramedics arrived. He was taken to Gosford hospital in a serious condition, and later transferred to Royal North Shore hospital.

The young man was in a critical but stable condition on Sunday morning, a hospital spokesperson said.

Witnesses said while there was storm activity visible in the distance towards Gosford, the area around the beach was clear and sunny and that the lightning bolt appeared to come “out of nowhere”.

Lightning can strike up to 30km from the centre of a storm cell.

Sunday was expected to be “another big storm day”, with “very dangerous and severe thunderstorms” likely for north-eastern NSW, said Sarah Scully, senior meteorologist at the Bureau of Meteorology.

Those storms could bring hail with a diameter greater than 5cm, and winds in excess of 120km/h.

“That’s being driven by a southerly change that’s pushing up the New South Wales coast,” Scully said. “It’s a potential trigger for these supercell high-end thunderstorms.”

Severe thunderstorms with 2cm hail, wind gusts in excess of 90km/h, heavy rainfall and flash flooding was possible on Sunday through a broader area of NSW extending down towards inland parts of the ranges to the east of Sydney, and pushing up into south-east and central parts of Queensland.

Storms were expected in Sydney on Sunday, but were likely to be less severe than further north, though rain was likely. The forecast temperature was 23C. Showers and thunderstorms were expected in Brisbane with a top of 31C.

The BoM expected to release more severe weather warnings throughout Sunday as the storms develop.

On Monday, the risk of high-end severe thunderstorms would move into the NSW northern rivers and south-eastern parts of Queensland, including Brisbane, the Gold Coast and the Sunshine Coast, again with the potential to bring giant hail, destructive winds, and intense rain that could lead to life-threatening flash flooding.

“That again is being driven by that trough that’s pushing up the coast combined with the very humid conditions that eastern Australia has been experiencing the last few days,” Scully said.

A coastal trough with a low-pressure system appeared to be developing off the north coast of NSW near the Queensland border, which had the potential to bring even more rain onto the coast.

“How much rainfall accumulates is highly dependent on how close that trough sits to the coast, and also how long it lingers there,” Scully said.

“At this state we’re only expecting moderate rainfall totals during tomorrow from that coastal trough, between 30mm to 70mm, easing into Tuesday morning. But we are watching it very closely as rainfall totals associated with these coastal troughs are notoriously challenging to predict. If they just move that little bit closer to the coast they can sometimes dump a whole lot of rain.”

Elsewhere, there were showers developing in Melbourne on Sunday with a forecast top of 17C. There was a high chance of showers in Hobart, with 13C expected, and cloudy in Adelaide with a mild 20C forecast. It was partly cloudy in Canberra, where they were expecting 24C, and in Perth, with an expected top of 28C. Darwin was expecting 34C and an afternoon storm.

As peace talks between Ukraine and Russia continue, Ukrainians are facing a rolling schedule of planned power cuts because of intensified Russian attacks on their energy grid. Ukraine has responded by striking Russian oil refineries.

Officials are concerned that life will quickly become unsustainable for millions of Ukrainians if power supplies cannot be maintained for at least four hours per day. Some privately warn that's close already, as some families are living with outages of up to 20 hours in Kyiv.

Ukraine correspondent Dan Johnson visits one of the country's power stations on the front lines of the war. Devastated by a direct ballistic missile strike, repair work is hampered by frequent air raids.

#88. Katherine Deves among four NSW upper house candidates revealed by One Nation

Party’s NSW leader Mike Newman says contenders are ‘people from the real world’ and not ‘career politicians’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The failed federal Liberal candidate Katherine Deves, who campaigned to exclude transgender women from women’s sport, will stand for One Nation at next year’s New South Wales election.

Deves was among four NSW Legislative Council candidates announced by One Nation’s NSW leader, Mike Newman, on Sunday.

The other candidates were Arnon Wither, a construction worker from the Hunter region who works as an electorate officer for Senator Sean Bell; Manti Steele, a farmer, teacher and mother of eight from Dubbo; and Daniel Hill, a surf lifesaver from the state’s south coast.

The NSW election will be held on 13 March.

Newman said on Sunday the upper house candidates were “authentic”, “people from the real world” and not “career politicians”.

One Nation has only announced a single lower house candidate – Newman, in the electorate of Cessnock – but he said the party would run candidates in all 93 lower house seats.

“We want to make sure that these people bleed orange, and like I said, they’re all from the real world,” he said.

“That’s the that’s the type of person we’re aiming at … people from the real world who understand business, who understand their pinch points and pain points, understand the cost of living, understand cashflow.”

Deves was handpicked by the former prime minister Scott Morrison to run in the federal NSW seat of Warringah in 2022. Her candidacy was marred by controversy, including when she likened anti-trans activism to standing up against the Holocaust.

On Sunday she said she joined One Nation “because I wanted to be a part of the rescue, recovery, and restoration of our nation”.

“We are seeing cost of living skyrocket, whether it’s fuel, housing, energy, and we are watching a government that is so wedded to destructive ideologies like DEI and net zero that they are prepared to impoverish us all,” she said.

If elected, she would look to make changes to state laws regarding “discrimination, hate speech, vilification, birth certificates, etc”.

“Where we can restore clear and accurate language around biological sex, men and women, and ensure that women and girls are safe and have spaces for services, sports, etc, where it’s inappropriate for males to be there.”

One Nation has required candidates at state elections to sign non-disclosure agreements preventing them from discussing their potential candidacy with the media.

News24 reported last month that Deves would run as an upper house candidate in the NSW election. She announced that she joined One Nation in September.

When Guardian Australia contacted Deves about her potential candidacy in October, she directed inquiries to Vikki Campion, the NSW party’s part-time media adviser. The party provided a statement saying it had confirmed only three candidates at that time – Newman, the lead upper house candidate, Stuart Bonds, and a Tamworth firearms manufacturer, Jeff Bacon.

#87. Asian love ballads test Eurovision’s winning song formula.

Carmelle of the Philippines defeated 10 competitors at the first edition of the high-camp event in Asia, held in Bangkok.

The Eurovision Song Contest crowned its first Asia champion in Bangkok on Saturday, in an event that tested whether a high-camp singing competition with an ardent fan base could appeal to audiences in a new market.

Carmelle of the Philippines was declared the winner of the inaugural Eurovision Song Contest Asia, beating out artists and original songs from 10 other countries: Bangladesh, Bhutan, Cambodia, Laos, Malaysia, Mongolia, Nepal, South Korea, Thailand and Vietnam. The show was live-streamed on YouTube and broadcast by television stations across the region.

Eurovision has been a fixture of the European cultural calendar since it began in 1956. Past winners include Sweden’s ABBA and the Canadian chanteuse Celine Dion, who represented Switzerland in 1988.

Asian artists have readily embraced the kitschy outfits and spectacular sets that are part of Eurovision tradition, with costumes that include traditional dress, as well as feathered capes and sequined corsets. Malaysia’s performer plans to enter the stage in a giant teardrop.

But the Eurovision format has not been tested in Asia. Fans and regional partners have expressed concern in recent weeks that budget constraints, production issues and weak audience demand could imperil the spinoff. Organizers say the contest could take time to build an audience.

“It would be profoundly arrogant to think that just because we have a heritage brand and a huge global audience for the European show, that we would walk in and demand it to be the same here,” said Martin Green, the European Broadcasting Union’s director of the Eurovision Song Contest. “Like any new thing, it needs to find its roots.”

Two previous attempts to take the competition to Asia were abandoned by the European Broadcasting Union, which owns Eurovision, and its partners in the region. A Eurovision spinoff in the United States, NBC’s American Song Contest, ran for one season in 2022.

The Asian edition kicking off on Saturday is far smaller than its European counterpart. Eurovision organizers said they were in talks with more countries to join future seasons. They include China, Japan and India, whose music industries have global reach.

“Bringing the world together through music is a fundamentally good idea,” Mr. Green said. “Europe shouldn’t keep it to itself.”

Ballads: Nine of the competition’s 11 participating countries picked performers singing about love in a wide range of styles, from ballads to a cappella hip-hop. Their choices suggest that the bombastic music often associated with victors of the European edition might not be a winning formula for Asian viewers. Read more ›

Fans: Our reporter spoke to some of the fans who traveled to Bangkok for Eurovision. Read more ›

Politics: Geopolitics have long intruded on the European edition of the Eurovision Song Contest, but they were not really a factor as the event prepared to expand into Asia. Read more ›

No shows: For a competition meant to introduce the diversity of Asian pop music to the world, the lineup for the first Eurovision Song Contest Asia has several notable absences, starting with China and India. Read more ›

As Thailand’s Mang and Daou waited to learn whether they had won, the 26-year-old Mang was shown onscreen crying as if she couldn’t take the tension. That’s the sort of emotion needed to make Eurovision’s expansion into Asia a success. Acts have to want the title. But the show will also need the big Asian nations -- India, Japan and China -- to buy in, and much could now depend on the reaction in the Philippines to Carmelle’s win. The event was far less spectacular than a typical Eurovision in Europe. It also featured far too many ballads for my taste, but it was a fun afternoon’s viewing.

Shiesam Flores, 27, a clerk from the eastern region of Bicol in the Philippines, has been watching the show at a party with her friends — and hoping that Carmelle would prevail.

“It was nerve-wracking, nail biting suspense!” she said. “But we know she would win! What a talent!”

The Philippines’ Carmelle wins the first Eurovision Song Contest Asia, pushing Thailand’s Mang and Daou into second.

The crowd roared in disappointment as Malaysia got only 70 points from the public vote.

Thailand takes a massive lead thanks to the public vote, but the genius of this voting process is we won’t know whether the Philippines can overcome them until the last seconds.

The public gave Beatmosphere, from Bangladesh, just 19 points. They looked surprisingly happy after getting such a low total, and it’s now confirmed they’ve come in a distant last.

Dara has brought the Eurovision Asia trophy to the stage. It’s a sparkly gold microphone.

The public vote can be brutal, as some acts rapidly learn that few viewers liked them.

The jury votes are all in, and it’s close. The Philippines is first with 93 points, Vietnam second with 83 points and Malaysia third with 82 points. We’re about to learn how many points each country’s act received from the public, starting with the current last place act, Bangladesh’s beatboxing-heavy crew Beatmosphere. It has 16 points.

One of the best parts of Eurovision is how the public’s choice is often completely diffferent from those of the juries, so don’t worry if your favorite act isn’t top of the ranking after the jury votes are all in. The Philippine’s jury, the first we’ve heard from, just gave its 12 points to Malaysia. That doesn’t mean it’ll win

Fans are decked out in flags and sequins for the first-ever Asia Eurovision here in Bangkok.

The problem Eurovision has with interval acts like Jeff Satur is that their stagecraft, singing ability and melodies are often a million times better than the artists in competition. Satur, who has 1.4 million monthly listeners on Spotify and over five million Instagram fans, has dozens of camp dancers onstage and a statue of a dragon. He would have cruised to victory if he’d been in competition, especially because of his ability to hold high notes close to the range only dogs can hear.

Simon Robinson, one of the fans who traveled to Bangkok for the Eurovision Song Contest on Saturday, said that his history with the competition is nearly as long as its seven-decade run.

He was eight years old when he first watched it with his family in Yorkshire, England, when Britain's Mary Hopkin finished in second place behind Ireland’s Dana.

“I really liked the concept of bringing Europe together after the war,” said Mr. Robinson, 63, who now lives in Melbourne, Australia. He described the contest’s annual broadcast as a social activity for his family and neighbors.

“Eurovision helped to create the European identity,” he added.

Other fans had their own motivations for traveling to see the contest’s first Asian edition.

Some are Europeans living in Asia, on the hunt for nostalgia, while others are Americans who were eager to experience their first Eurovision.

Lee Hudson, an American television executive based in Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia, said that he gets emotional when he talks about Eurovision, which he has watched since 2012.

“The only reason I know any music from European countries is because I heard them on Eurovision,” he said. “Eurovision Asia makes sense because the musical cultures are distinct.”

Mr. Hudson said he still listens to Loreen’s “Euphoria,” which won the competition for Sweden in 2012.

It was also the first Eurovision that Drew Andrea watched from his home in Sydney. A year later, he got a passport to fly to Sweden for the 2013 edition, and has attended every final since. Coming to Bangkok for Eurovision Asia was much more affordable, he said.

“Asia understands the brief — the kitschy costumes, the dance music,” he said.

His top picks among the Asian performers were the Philippines’ Carmelle and Vietnam’s Toc Tien. “I think they hit the nail on the head with the song, staging, costume and traditional elements,” he said.

Luca Cian, a professor of marketing at the University of Virginia who grew up in Italy, said that Eurovision built a global fan base by offering a shared cultural experience.

“Millions of people across Europe watch it at the same moment,” he said. “Families have watch parties with food and drinks. There are public discussions. Rituals create community.”

Carmen Chung, a British accountant, said she hijacked her family’s holiday in Bangkok to attend Eurovision Asia with her brother Garry Chung, a banker who lives in Hong Kong. “We were raised on Eurovision,” she said of their childhood in Yorkshire.

European acts, she said, tend to copy whatever style of music won the previous year. “This year, because there are no expectations, everyone has just done their own thing,” she said. She and her brother said they were rooting for Bangladesh’s Beatmosphere, a beatboxing group that will perform an a cappella love song.

Martin Green, the director of the Eurovision Song Contest, said that about half of the tickets for Saturday’s final were purchased by “super fans” from outside Asia. Organizers have kept the inaugural show modest, with 11 participating countries and a 5,000-seat venue.

Mr. Andrea said he didn’t mind the small scale.

“I’m O.K. with humble beginnings as long as it keeps growing every year,” he said.

The Thai pop star Jeff Satur, a guest performer, got the high-camp memo.

The presenters are talking to the artists in the green room and trying to get them to talk excitedly about Bulgaria, the country that will host the 2027 Eurovision Song Contest. The winner tonight might get to perform at that event. That seems to be making this show less about Asia itself than a spin-off of the main Eurovision event

Eurovision voting involves two parts. Juries representing music industry professionals from each country are allocated half the votes, and the viewing public decides the other half. To try to build tension, the show always announces the jury votes first by going live to representatives from each country who reveals their top acts. In the European edition of the contest, some of those spokespeople are celebrities, and often they try to give mini speeches or make jokes. This part of the show is always awful. You have been warned.

There are a lot of European expatriates in this crowd. That much is obvious when Bulgaria’s Dara, the winner of this year’s Eurovision, takes the stage with her winning song, “Bangaranga.” Some fans even danced along with her choreography!

Mohamad Iqbal Dzulqarnain, an Aina Abdul fan in Kuala Lumpur, said he felt her performance was better than the recording version.

“She left no crumbs,” he said. “She is going to win the contest.”

For a competition meant to introduce the diversity of Asian pop music to the world, the lineup for the first Eurovision Song Contest Asia on Saturday had several notable absences.

For starters, the continent’s four most populous nations — China, India, Pakistan and Indonesia — were not there. Even Japan, a country globally considered a cultural powerhouse, was absent.

That left some of the region’s best-known musical cultures out of the mix. India has Bollywood hits. China has produced both Mandopop and Cantopop, the genre associated with Hong Kong pop stars. JPop was once huge across Asia.

As a result, the only large pop powerhouse in this contest was South Korea.

China’s absence is relatively simple to explain. Performers are entered by local broadcasters, and Eurovision has long had a messy relationship with China’s, which are heavily regulated and try to reflect the government’s social and political values. Mango TV once had a deal to broadcast the high-camp Eurovision to Chinese viewers, but Eurovision abruptly canceled it in 2018 after Mango TV censored acts whose performances featured gay themes. It also blurred rainbow flags in the performance arena.

Frank-Dieter Freiling, a former Eurovision official, said in an interview that such censorship was against Eurovision’s values and that the show’s organizers had historically barred broadcasters from airing the competition with delays.

It’s less clear why broadcasters from India, Pakistan, Japan and Indonesia did not participate. Assaf Blecher, Eurovision Asia’s executive producer, said that conversations with Chinese, Japanese and Indian broadcasters had not concluded in time. But he insisted that they “are knocking on our door.”

Not participating saved those countries the potential embarrassment of being the regional power that comes in last.

Just look to Europe, where Britain and Germany often perform badly at Eurovision. In this year’s show, Britain’s Look Mum No Computer placed last, scoring only one point, while Germany finished third from last. Whenever that happens, political commentators give interviews suggesting that the results reflect those countries’ poor standing across Europe.

Here at the watch party in Kathmandu, Pratik’s relatives are happy with his performance.

“His voice was cracking during the selection round in Nepal,” said Sunita Rai, his sister-in-law. “Today’s performance was much more powerful.”

His relatives have been on social media all night, urging their friends who live overseas to vote for him. (You can’t vote for your own country’s performer.)

The presenters have just announced a technical issue! When Laos’s Thinla performed earlier, she had an earpiece problem and was unable to hear her own music. So that explains why she sang so out of tune. They’re now playing a recording of an earlier performance to everyone to try to ensure the event is fair

Rizal Kamal, a senior adviser to the Arts, Live Festival and Events Association, an entertainment nonprofit based in Malaysia, said he felt that Aina Abdul was the right singer to represent the country at Eurovision.

“Aina is seasoned in major competitions and she knows how to wow both the judges and the audience,” he said. “I am filled with pride.”

Aina Abdul’s inventive wardrobe and her powerful voice sets her apart from the Asian peers, said Adriana Syuhaidah, a fan in Kuala Lumpur.

“She is the Lady Gaga of Malaysia,” she said.

When they sat down to plan the final show of the inaugural Eurovision Song Contest in Asia, the organizers faced a challenge: How could they introduce variety when most of the entries were slow-paced love songs?

Nine of the competition’s 11 participating countries picked performers singing about love in a wide range of styles, from ballads to a cappella hip-hop. Their choices suggest that the bombastic music often associated with victors of the European edition might not be a winning formula for Asian viewers.

“Europeans tend to prefer songs with a crescendo, a hook and a grand ending, but also a strong social message,” said Dean Vuletic, a historian of contemporary Europe at the University of Luxembourg who is an authority on Eurovision.

“The audience doesn’t want to see an innocuous pop song,” he said.

Electro-pop and dance songs have featured heavily on the list of Eurovision winners over the past decade. In May, Bulgaria’s Dara won with “Bangaranga,” a dance-pop song inspired by her diagnosis with attention deficit hyperactive disorder. Nemo, from Switzerland, won in 2024 with an EDM opera about their nonbinary identity, while in 2018, Israel’s Netta won with “Toy,” a pop anthem about female independence.

Some countries hold a nationwide contest to select an artist and a song to represent them in Eurovision, while others leave the choice to Eurovision’s local broadcasting partner. Audiences in the Philippines voted for Carmelle in a televised competition, for example, while Vietnam Television chose Toc Tien.

The two are considered front-runners in the Asian edition, with their high-energy folk-pop songs about female empowerment.

Malaysia’s Aina Abdul, another early favorite, will showcase her powerful vocals in “Bluest Sky,” a ballad with an orchestral arrangement that is about a troubled relationship. The track is reminiscent of “Wasted Love,” an opera-techno song that won Austria the European title in 2025.

Pratik Himal Rai of Nepal, who got married this summer, said love was easy to sing about because it can be understood across borders and languages.

“It is love that holds the world together,” he said in an interview.

He will sing “Ananta Maya” in Nepali. Other Eurovision Asia artists blend English lyrics with their national languages.

In Europe, every winning song after 2022 has been in English. That year’s champion, Ukraine's Kalush Orchestra, sang in their own language on “Stefania,” a hip-hop track that interspersed a Ukrainian lullaby with rap verses and traditional woodwind instruments.

Winning songs tend to blend a country’s national instruments and musical motifs with internationally trendy pop, Mr. Vuletic said.

A traditional Bhutanese flute can be heard in “Lhayi Semo,” Nyendra’s love ballad. Monetrey’s “I Let You Go” uses a Cambodian bamboo xylophone and a tro, a string instrument played with a bow. A kulintang, a percussion instrument made of eight brass gongs, keeps the fast beat in Carmelle’s “Sobra-Sobra.”

#85. Anthony Albanese apologises for hot-mic gaffe after Coalition criticises ‘locker-room talk’

Prime minister says he and Chris Bowen were having ‘light-hearted banter’ when they were caught discussing where they keep their phones

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Anthony Albanese has apologised for a hot-mic gaffe at the UN climate talks in Turkey, saying he regretted being overheard discussing mobile phone radiation and “dicks” in a candid conversation.

“I of course regret that. I apologise,” Albanese said during a visit to the Australian warship HMAS Stalwart in the Philippines on Sunday.

“This was a private conversation, we thought, between two friends having a bit of light-hearted banter.”

Ahead of a meeting at the Cop31 climate summit in Antalya on Friday, Albanese and the climate change minister, Chris Bowen, were caught on camera discussing which pocket they use to keep their mobile phone.

Albanese can be heard telling Bowen that he should store his phone away from his heart because “it can interfere with your rhythm”. Laughing, Bowen asks whether the advice was because a phone can be “a little bit radioactive”.

Albanese says “it makes absolute sense”, before Bowen replies that he was more conscious of putting his phone in his pants pocket. The prime minister asks: “Because of your dick?”

Bowen agrees and says: “Arguably more important than the heart.”

On Sunday, Albanese played down the incident which occurred ahead of a meeting with business leaders in Antalya.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

“No one was hurt or harmed. We weren’t having private banter which criticised anyone,” he said.

The speedy apology is in contrast to previous gaffes by Albanese, including long-running controversy over comments about the gift of melons from Japan’s prime minister, Sanae Takaichi, on a podcast.

Albanese previously apologised for saying he would “shag” Australian singer Kylie Minogue in the same episode of the Bush Deep podcast.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

The melons comment risked becoming an international incident, as Albanese repeatedly insisted the comments and his corresponding hand gestures were not a reference to breasts.

Japan’s government sent a diplomatic cable to Australia in July, days after the podcast originally aired, claiming there had been “sensationalised reporting in the Australian media” and saying they detected no “ill intention” in Albanese’s statement.

The Coalition on Sunday criticised Albanese and Bowen’s comments.

Opposition frontbencher James Paterson said the remarks were unfortunate, telling News24 they could damage impressions of Australia on the world stage.

“That locker-room talk like that has a time and a place and it’s not at international summits when you’re the prime minister of Australia talking to your energy minister,” he said.

But the employment minister, Amanda Rishworth, dismissed the criticism, saying the comments were a joke.

“This is a private conversation in between meetings,” she said.

“I know that a hot-mic incident is very exciting for people ... but really, there are bigger issues to tackle and discuss.”

Earlier a spokesperson for the prime minister said the hot-mic comments were “a joke in between meetings”.

Albanese is in the Philippines for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit and high-level diplomatic meetings, including with the US president, Donald Trump.

He will hold formal bilateral talks with the Filipino president, Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr, on Sunday and visited a Jollibee fast food restaurant to celebrate the chain’s expansion to Australia.

#83. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#80. China rules out hosting Cop33 climate summit in 2028

Decision disappoints experts hoping for world’s biggest emitter to take global lead on climate crisis

China has ruled out hosting the Cop33 UN climate summit in 2028 after months of deliberations, leaving the field open for other Asian countries to hold it, the Guardian understands.

The decision will disappoint many countries and climate experts, who had hoped the world’s biggest emitter would take the lead on the global stage, as the US under Donald Trump has vacated all forms of international cooperation on the climate.

Al Gore, the former US vice-president, was among those left disheartened by the decision. “I would be very much in favour of that idea [of China hosting the summit],” he told the Guardian. “It’s too bad.”

China is in the midst of an economic transformation, from being heavily dependent on coal to becoming an “electro state”. John Kerry, the former US secretary of state, told the Guardian that China was leaving the rest of the world behind in forging and using the low-carbon technologies of the future.

Renewable energy generation capacity has now outstripped coal in China, and its greenhouse gas emissions appear to have declined slightly this year.

Sales of electric vehicles in the country have also taken off, making up nearly two-thirds of the market last month, and China’s clean energy industries drove more than 90% of the country’s investment growth last year.

The rules of the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) mean an Asian country should host in 2028.

Gareth Redmond-King, the international lead at the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit thinktank, said China’s position as an industrial powerhouse would have made it a formidable contender to hold the summit.

“It would undoubtedly be significant for the world’s biggest emitter and second-biggest economy to host a climate Cop,” he said.

“They’re leading the world on investment, manufacture and deployment of clean energy tech, and their emissions have started to tick down. As a leadership story, China hosting Cop33 would be a big deal.”

It is not clear why China has decided against hosting the conference, but several experts that spoke to the Guardian suggested that the US electoral cycle was likely to have been a factor. The next US presidential election will take place on 7 November 2028, and Cop33 is scheduled for 6 to 17 November that year.

After the strong showing by Democrats in the recent midterm elections, in which voter concern over the climate crisis was thought to be a factor, the Democrats will be hoping to elect a climate-friendly president in 2028. But even if they succeed, Trump will still be in office until the following January.

The US president rejects climate science and withdrew the US from the Paris agreement, and is also withdrawing from its parent treaty, the 1992 UNFCCC.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

China seeks to be seen as a leader among developing countries, but has been wary of stepping into the gap left by the US. Under the Democratic presidents Joe Biden and Barack Obama, the US and China enjoyed a close partnership on climate issues, setting joint targets on reducing methane and developing clean technology.

Cop33 will be an important meeting because it will include the “global stocktake” – a mechanism under the 2015 Paris climate agreement that examines countries’ progress on cutting emissions in line with the goal of limiting global temperature rises to 1.5C.

That limit is now certain to be surpassed, but experts say countries should now try to minimise the temperature “overshoot”, to avoid worse ravages from extreme weather.

India’s government considered offering to host Cop33 for several months earlier this year, but also decided against it. India’s coal use has been increasing, even as the country exceeds its own renewable energy targets.

The Guardian understands that other countries mulling a bid to host Cop33 include Uzbekistan, South Korea, Indonesia and Thailand.

No decision on a host is likely to be made at this year’s Cop31 meeting, which is ongoing in Turkey, with the Turkish and Australian governments jointly presiding over the summit.

#76. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#70. Nvidia scandal: US treasury chief blames Lutnick’s commerce department over alleged lack of action

US commerce secretary faces sack as questions emerge over senior Trump officials’ knowledge of alleged Chinese spy’s ties to Jensen Huang

The US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, is facing growing pressure over his apparent lack of action over the Nvidia spying scandal as other Trump administration officials publicly raise questions over his decisions.

Scott Bessent, the US treasury secretary, blamed Lutnick’s commerce department for the fact the US had not imposed export restrictions on certain Nvidia chips to China.

Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday, and was replaced on an interim basis by the company’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, after reports emerged of his multiple affairs, most importantly with Alice Huang (no relation), who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

White House officials are discussing whether Lutnick should be fired, the Associated Press reported, after it emerged that a US intelligence report about Huang’s relationship with the alleged spy was circulated among senior figures in the Trump administration.

Lutnick is now scheduled to testify before Congress next month, just days after the ousted Nvidia chief answers lawmakers’ questions on Capitol Hill.

Lutnick is also facing criticism for a delay by the commerce department in adding Singapore-based Megaspeed, which is associated with Alice Huang and has bought billions of dollars of Nvidia chips in recent years, to Washington’s blacklist of companies that the US considers a risk to national security despite reported concerns within the administration.

Bessent has “for months” been in favor of putting Megaspeed on the blacklist – but Lutnick’s commerce department “has the pen”, Bessent told CNBC. “You will have to ask them about their internal process,” he added in an interview with the network on Friday.

John Curtis, a Utah Republican senator, said that Lutnick’s reported delay on export controls regarding Megaspeed “raises the question of whether the commerce department is acting in the best interests of the American people”.

The Republican senator John Cornyn told the Financial Times that there needed to be “a thorough investigation and accountability for the outrageous circumvention of national security export controls”, adding: “Heads should roll.”

The Senate Democratic leader, Chuck Schumer, said Jensen Huang and Nvidia must answer as to whether US security had been jeopardised, adding that the Trump administration also must account for what it did once it learned about his reported relationship with Alice Huang.

“Secretary Lutnick has proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the banking committee to address their questions,” Kristen Eichamer, a spokesperson for Lutnick, told NBC News.

Trump’s close ties with Jensen Huang have also come under renewed scrutiny as a timeline of the saga started to emerge. The president called him two days before he stepped down and urged him to fight for his job, the New York Times has reported.

On Thursday, Schumer questioned what Trump knew when he personally invited Jensen Huang aboard Air Force One on a state visit to Beijing earlier this year, adding: “Americans deserve to know that those at the highest levels of power are putting America first, not their bottom line or their personal friendships.”

The House Democratic leader, Hakeem Jeffries, said he had demanded a classified briefing for all members of Congress about Nvidia.

Republican Tim Scott, the Senate banking committee chair, said he had sent letters asking Lutnick and Jensen Huang to appear before him, citing “recent reports [that] raise serious ‌concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology”.

The statement from Nvidia’s board of directors confirming Huang’s departure did not mention the spying scandal and personal-conduct revelations, instead paying tribute to his “profound” contributions to the company he cofounded.

Huang is reported to have had relationships with Alice Huang as well as an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, according to the New York Times, and another, unnamed employee, according to Reuters.

The House foreign affairs chair, Brian Mast, a Republican, said on Thursday that congressional staff had spoken to Nvidia on Wednesday night about a date and location for Huang’s testimony.

The House speaker, Mike Johnson, said on Thursday there was now growing consensus in Congress on limiting chip exports to China.

Nvidia’s advanced AI chips are widely used to develop cutting-edge AI models, making the company important to the US race against ​China to build superior AI systems. The US has sought for years to ‌limit China’s access to Nvidia’s chips because it does ‌not want the Chinese military to benefit from them.

“The export controls on chips to China has been a big issue around here,” Johnson told reporters ‌at the US Capitol, adding that he favoured limiting China’s access to the advanced technology. “I think there’s a developing consensus in Congress that is an important position to take.“

Chinese AI companies are now trying to secure Nvidia chip capacity outside China, amid growing concern that the gap in US export controls may now be closed after the scandal, people familiar with the matter told Reuters.

Beijing has also stepped up efforts to develop domestic alternatives, including through Huawei, although analysts say Chinese chips remain less efficient for training advanced AI models.

Lawmakers are pressing their colleagues to pass at least four bills on chip exports, according to statements issued since Monday.

On Chinese social media, many expressed surprise at what they see as Huang’s swift resignation, and mourned the sudden end of an era that lasted for more than three decades.

News of his resignation attracted more than 400m posts overnight on the Chinese microblogging platform Weibo.

Huang had enjoyed rock-star status in China, where his visits drew crowds seeking selfies and autographs and his casual outings became social-media sensations. During a January 2026 visit, opportunistic sellers advertised photographs with him for 25,000 yuan ($3,700), while a Beijing restaurant launched a special menu costing more than 400 yuan after he ate there.

#64. Hungarian authorities uncover $20m during probe of Orbán-era minister

The seized money has been deposited into the country's national treasury

Nearly $20m (Â£14.8m) in cash has been found hidden in farm buildings and buried in forests as part of an investigation into a former minister of Viktor OrbÃ¡n, authorities have said.

MiklÃ³s SesztÃ¡k, who served as minister of national development for four of the ex-prime minister's 16-year rule, is accused of accepting bribes as part of a scheme involving the state transport company. He has denied any wrongdoing.

Hungary's new Prime Minister PÃ©ter Magyar unseated OrbÃ¡n in a landslide election in April on the promise of launching a sweeping anti-corruption drive.

On Friday, following the two-day search for the cash - found in secluded locations across the country - Magyar described it as the end of the "mafia".

Investigators found wads of banknotes in safes, buried in woodlands and hidden in agricultural and residential buildings and warehouses, as part of the probe into SesztÃ¡k, the National Asset Recovery and Protection Office (NVVH) said in a statement.

The cash amounted to "5,071,632,000 forints, â¬2,658,350 and $1,257,100", it added, bringing the combined value to $19.5m. It has been deposited into the Hungarian State Treasury, the body said.

The NVVH was established to investigate allegations of corruption during the OrbÃ¡n rule, which Magyar has dubbed "Operation Purgatory".

Magyar promised Hungarians "regime change" during the election earlier this year and has set about undoing many OrbÃ¡n-era policies since entering office.

Allegations of corruption and cronyism have long dogged OrbÃ¡n, who during the course of his tenure turned Hungary into what the EU characterised as an "electoral autocracy".

Hungarian investigators said they found some of the cash buried in forestry

His government faced accusations of offering lucrative government projects to allies. OrbÃ¡n has strongly denied the claims.

Two of his former ministers, including SesztÃ¡k, and the former central bank chief Gyorgy Matolcsy have been under arrest since September over accusations of graft in separate investigations. They all deny wrongdoing.

On Monday, Hungarian authorities also detained OrbÃ¡n's brother and three others as part of a people-smuggling case.

#60. Nigeria cautions against travel via Ethiopia's main airport due to altitude

Bole International Airport is a major travel hub

Nigeria's government has cautioned its citizens, in particular those with medical conditions, against travelling via Ethiopia's main airport due to its altitude.

The foreign ministry in Abuja said 16 Nigerians with underlying health problems had died this year while transiting through Addis Ababa.

The city sits at an altitude of 2,334m (7,657ft) above sea level, making it one of the highest capitals in the world. Bole International Airport is a major hub for travel between Africa, the Middle East and Asia, serving millions of passengers a year.

Nigeria said those with cardiovascular and respiratory conditions were at an increased risk.

The Nigerian foreign ministry did not disclose the identities of the travellers it said had died, nor the cause of death.

It urged anyone with a health condition exacerbated by high altitudes to "exercise caution" when planning a journey via Addis Ababa and "obtain appropriate medical guidance" before doing so.

The ministry stressed that it did not want to discourage Nigerians from travelling through the Ethiopian capital.

Visiting an area at a high altitude can be dangerous for people with heart and lung diseases, as the air is thinner and so less oxygen can get into the bloodstream, experts say.

This means the heart has to work harder to get enough oxygen to different parts of the body, which can sometimes lead to heart failure among those with existing conditions, according to the American Heart Association.

The lack of oxygen at high altitudes can cause adverse effects such as dizziness, fatigue and shortness of breath among healthy people - which can cause complications for people with lung disease, studies show.

#58. Philippines’ Carmelle wins first Eurovision contest in Asia

Filipino entry, pop and rap song Sobra-Sobra, takes 19-year-old to victory at grand final in Bangkok

Carmelle Collado has won the first Eurovision song contest Asia, taking the Philippines’ entry, Sobra-Sobra, to victory in Bangkok on Saturday night, at the first regional edition of the contest to be staged outside Europe.

Eleven countries competed at the One Bangkok Forum in central Bangkok, and the show ran for two and a half hours including advertising.

The winner, who performs under the single name Carmelle, finished on 211 points, 41 clear of the hosts Thailand, with Vietnam third. She received the most votes from both the national juries and the public ballot.

Carmelle, 19, grew up beside the railway tracks in the Bicol region of south-eastern Luzon and has been competing in televised talent contests since she was 12.

Sobra-Sobra mixes pop and rap, is supported by traditional Philippine instruments, the kubing and kulintang, and is sung in Tagalog and English.

After her victory, Carmelle said her song celebrated female empowerment and was intended as “a tribute to all Filipino women”.

“I made history, since this was the first time,” she told the Associated Press. “It’s such a big responsibility for me, because this one is a global stage, so thank you everybody who voted for me.”

“It’s about how unapologetic, independent and strong Filipinas are,” she told the Guardian before the final. “It describes me – not just me, but of course all the Filipinas.”

The show opened with a routine celebrating the host country, in which Muay Thai fighters shared the stage with drag performers on street food carts. The 11 acts were then driven on in tuk-tuks, each carrying their national flag.

Vietnam’s Tóc Tiên, a star at home for more than 20 years, performed first, among dancers costumed as lotus leaves. Mang x Daou represented Thailand, a duo assembled for the contest in the host country who performed a duet staged like a musical number.

Nepal’s Pratik Himal Rai gave one of the most conventionally Eurovision performances of the night with Ananta Maya, a rock ballad staged with fire imagery and a large heart-shaped structure behind him.

He ended it by appealing for support for his country, which he said was “going [through a] very hard situation”. More than 1,500 people were killed and more than 5,000 were reported missing after a rock and ice avalanche near the Chinese border triggered catastrophic flash floods in August.

Bangladesh’s Beatmosphere, originally a nine-member a cappella and beatbox group, sang without a single instrument. Three of their members were left out of the final due to a rule capping each act at six performers on stage.

Nyendra from Bhutan sang dressed in the gho, his country’s national dress for men, against a backdrop of Himalayan peaks. Mongolia’s Beck performed in a reinterpreted deel, also a national costume.

Half the result was decided before the show began. The 11 national juries voted at a dress rehearsal on Friday night and put the Philippines at the top with 93 points, ahead of Vietnam and Malaysia.

The other half came from viewers around the world, who could vote online but not for their own country, and who were given eight free votes each and the option to buy 10 more.

Laos’s entrant, Thinla, was unable to hear her own music during her performance, and her dress rehearsal was aired once all live performances were over. She finished fifth, with the night’s third-highest public score.

Carmelle receives no prize money but does get a trophy presented by Dara, the Bulgarian singer who won this year’s European contest, and will perform at Eurovision 2027 in Burgas.

Organisers have said the right to stage future editions will not automatically go to the winner, on the grounds that smaller countries lack the infrastructure.

Some of the clearest echoes of Eurovision’s camp traditions came through those who announced the votes.

South Korea’s jury points were read by Jo Kwon, a veteran pop star who has played Adam in the Korean production of Priscilla, Queen of the Desert and the title role in Everybody’s Talking About Jamie.

The Philippines’ were read by Vice Ganda, a gay comedian who is one of the biggest stars on Philippine television.

Meanwhile, the guest performer was the Thai singer Jeff Satur, who reached a wider Asian audience through KinnPorsche, a 2022 series in the genre of male same-sex romance dramas that has a large following across Asia.

Organisers intend the Bangkok show to be the first of many regional editions, with the executive producer, Assaf Blecher, saying that 16 or 17 territories had expressed interest in competing next year, among them China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

#55. Arrested generals and a threatened VP: President's son fuels Ugandan succession fever

Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba has signalled his intention to run for president in the next elections

In Uganda's military, even senior generals are finding that their rank offers little protection from the wrath of the country's powerful army chief.

Over the past 15 months, at least a dozen generals and senior officers have been arrested under Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba, who is also the son of President Yoweri Museveni, in a crackdown that has unsettled the military's top ranks.

The arrests come amid growing political tensions over who will succeed Museveni, who has ruled Uganda for four decades.

Gen Kainerugaba, 52, is widely seen as a potential successor, although his ambitions appear to be facing resistance within the ruling party.

This week, he threatened Vice-President Jessica Alupo with dismissal and possible arrest, while suggesting he should take her position.

The ruling National Resistance Movement (NRM) has, however, rejected reports that its senior leadership had rejected a proposal to back Gen Kainerugaba as the next president, describing the claims as "completely false".

This has all fuelled speculation that the arrests are part of an effort to sideline military officers opposed to the general's presidential ambitions.

Others see the arrests as evidence of an anti-corruption drive in which senior commanders are held to account.

At the centre of the tension are two powerful powerful institutions: the military, where Gen Kainerugaba wields considerable influence, and the NRM, where questions about his political ambitions appear to be dividing senior figures.

A recent meeting of NRM's top leadership ended without agreement on a proposal to position Gen Kainerugaba as Museveni's successor, according to the AFP news agency.

Five party sources who attended the closed-door meeting told AFP that a vote was abandoned after several members spoke against the proposal.

Uganda's news website ChimpReports, however, said that most members who spoke backed the general's potential presidency, although some questioned his readiness for civilian leadership.

In a statement on Thursday, NRM Secretary General Richard Todwong Awany confirmed that the party had met on 10 November to discuss "political, social and economic issues".

He dismissed reports that the meeting had rejected a proposal to position Gen Kainerugaba as Uganda's next president, saying the party would communicate any decisions "through the right channel, at the right time".

President Yoweri Museveni's grandson (standing up, shaking the president's hand) has joined the army, alongside his father (sitting down, wearing camouflage)

Dr Gerald Bareebe, a Ugandan-born political scientist at Canada's York University, sees a connection between the arrests and political manoeuvring over Museveni's succession, often referred to as the "Muhoozi Project".

"Senior officers believed not to support the project have been arrested, detained or charged with corruption and treason," he tells the BBC.

He says the allegations against some officers were widely viewed as politically motivated, reflecting growing divisions within the military over the succession question.

Some of those detained have since been released, apparently after securing the general's forgiveness.

The detention of one of the country's most prominent former commanders, Lt Gen Peter Elwelu, has brought the crackdown into sharper focus.

The former deputy army chief was detained on 2 November over allegations that he had plotted to destabilise the eastern Teso region.

His fall from grace followed a series of social media posts by Kainerugaba, who accused him of treachery and announced that he had been stripped of his three-star rank and demoted.

"The traitor Peter Elwelu is hereby stripped of his ranks. He is demoted to Private," Kainerugaba wrote on X.

He also claimed that more than 20 soldiers linked to Elwelu had been arrested.

The announcement raised legal questions, with Ugandan media reporting that military regulations do not allow a senior officer to be stripped of his rank through a social media post.

The army later confirmed Elwelu's detention, saying they had recovered 15 guns, military uniforms and two pickup trucks linked to him. His family has rejected the allegations.

Elwelu is among at least a dozen generals and senior officers detained since April 2025, according to an investigation by Uganda's Daily Monitor newspaper.

They include three former military intelligence chiefs who once oversaw some of Uganda's most sensitive security operations.

This army chief threatened to castrate opposition leader and claimed descent from Jesus

The 'shadow army' helping Uganda's long-serving president keep an iron grip on power

The Ugandan general who lobs grenades on Twitter

Maj Gen James Birungi, a former head of defence intelligence and security, was arrested in August 2025, on allegations including treason, terrorism and corruption.

He was charged before a military court but later released after Gen Kainerugaba announced that he had "forgiven" him.

Another former intelligence chief, Maj Gen Abel Kandiho, was detained in September this year over allegations of providing false intelligence and treachery. He remains in detention, according to the Daily Monitor.

His predecessor, Brig-Gen Charles Bakahumura, was also arrested in September over similar allegations, but was later released after the army chief announced that he had forgiven him.

Other officers have faced accusations ranging from stealing military fuel to taking money to spy on military communications.

The military has defended the arrests as part of efforts to tackle corruption and criminality within the armed forces.

Maj Gen James Birungi was arrested in August 2025, on allegations including treason, terrorism and corruption

Ugandan security analyst Freddie David Egesa says the arrests reflect investigations involving different security agencies, rather than an army-led purge.

He argues that detaining senior officers helps protect witnesses and demonstrates the seriousness of the anti-corruption drive.

"Gen Muhoozi has the overall duty to permit the investigation team to arrest these high-profile people, in consultation with the commander-in-chief," Egesa says, adding: "The arrests are not summarily done."

But Bareebe sees a wider political motive, arguing that officers suspected of opposing to Gen Kainerugaba's presidential ambitions are being targeted.

"The result is a broad purge that has affected both serving and retired officers perceived to be anti-Muhoozi," he says.

He points to divisions between the elite Special Forces Command (SFC), which he says is largely loyal to the president's son, and army commanders who remain unconvinced about his succession.

The allegations against the officers are at different stages, with some facing charges, others in detention without publicly announced charges, and several having been released.

Bareebe also questions whether military trials can deliver independent proceedings.

"Many Ugandans regard the charges as politically motivated and therefore expect little justice," he adds.

He alleges that some officers have had charges dropped after closed-door negotiations with the president and his son, during which they were pressured to declare support for Gen Kainerugaba.

Government spokesperson Allan Kasujja dismissed the claims of a succession fallout as "hogwash".

Unlike his predecessors, who communicated through official military channels, Gen Kainerugaba frequently uses X to announce arrests, criticise public officials and comment on domestic and international affairs. Some have sparked diplomatic controversy.

On the night of 9 November, he turned his attention to Vice-President Jessica Alupo, demanding to take her job.

"Whoever is in that office should prepare to hand it over soon," he wrote, in posts that he later deleted.

He also questioned Alupo's loyalty and threatened her dismissal, adding that the government would operate "on military terms", if he became his father's deputy.

The following day, his X account, which had nearly 1.4 million followers, became inaccessible.

It was unclear whether he deactivated it himself, which he has done before, or the platform had suspended it.

Alupo remains in office and has yet to respond publicly.

Vice-President Jessica Alupo is yet to respond to Gen Kainerugaba's posts questioning her loyalty

Egesa says Gen Kainerugaba's controversial posts reflect his preferred style of communicating, but adds that security analysts are assessing their implications for Uganda's political and military stability.

Bareebe, however, sees them as part of a broader effort by the army chief to extend his influence into civilian politics.

He says the public confrontation with senior political figures illustrates how the succession debate was spilling beyond the army and into the ruling party.

Gen Kainerugaba was appointed army chief in March 2024, after previously commanding the SFC, which is responsible for protecting the president and strategic installations.

Under Uganda's military structure, he reports directly to Museveni, giving him considerable influence over the army.

Bareebe argues that the SFC's loyalty to the president and his son could prove crucial in any future transfer of power.

"The regular army remains important but has been sidelined by the SFC, which now functions as a personal army for Museveni and his son," he adds.

At the heart of the tension is the question of who will succeed Museveni, 82, who has ruled Uganda since 1986, when his rebel National Resistance Army seized power after a five-year guerrilla war.

He was sworn in for a seventh elected presidential term earlier this year, extending his rule into a fifth decade.

Gen Kainerugaba has been regarded as a possible successor and has indicated that he intends to seek the presidency in 2031, when his father's current term is due to end.

He also leads the Patriotic League of Uganda, a political movement that has mobilised support around his leadership.

But recent developments suggest that a transfer of power from father to son may be more complicated than his supporters anticipated.

At the recent meeting of the NRM leadership, one official reportedly urged Museveni to retire his son from the military and give him a civilian role to gain administrative experience, AFP reported.

Local media reported that some party officials consider Gen Salim Saleh, Museveni's younger brother, better suited for civilian leadership.

There is, however, no indication that he is interested in becoming president.

Others reportedly defended Gen Kainerugaba, pointing to his military leadership, anti-corruption campaign and grassroots political mobilisation.

Bareebe says opposition to the proposed succession has grown within the NRM, with both veteran figures and younger members concerned about what they see as an attempt to secure power through coercion.

He suggests Museveni's reluctance to move his son out of the military could reflect the importance of retaining control of the army during any transition.

"The underlying calculation appears to be that, so long as Muhoozi controls the SFC, he can use it to capture power even in an event of a contested transition," he says.

The military has been central to Museveni's hold on power, making the loyalties of senior commanders particularly important as succession questions intensify.

The family's military influence has also drawn attention to the possibility of a political dynasty spanning generations, with Gen Kainerugaba's son, Ruhamya, recently graduating from Britain's Sandhurst military academy.

The succession debate is unfolding against a backdrop of sustained pressure on the opposition.

Veteran opposition politician Kizza Besigye remains in detention on treason charges, which he denies.

Bobi Wine, who came second to Museveni in this year's presidential election, has gone into exile in the US, after being threatened by Gen Kainerugaba.

The government has consistently defended its security operations as lawful and necessary to maintain stability.

But the arrests, his son's threats and competing accounts of divisions within the ruling party have intensified questions about how Uganda's next political transition might unfold, and what role his family may play.

He once criticised African leaders who cling to power. Now he's won a seventh term

#54. World leaders head to Asia for back-to-back diplomatic summits

Tussle for influence as trade, tech and regional security to take centre stage in China, Philippines and Cambodia meetings

The global political centre of gravity will be firmly in Asia this week as world leaders fly in for back-to-back high-level summits against the backdrop of the tussle for influence between the superpowers China and the US.

Beijing will have the home advantage when it welcomes Donald Trump to its southern technology hub of Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) leaders’ meeting. It starts two weeks after the US leader lost midterm elections.

The summit also comes after an awkward-at-best revelation that the head of Nvidia, the US chipmaker that for a long time was the world’s most valuable company, was having an affair with a woman US intelligence suspects was a Chinese spy.

Apec represents more than 60% of global GDP and has primarily promoted free trade and multilateralism. This year’s meeting could be defined, however, by the confirmed trilateral meeting between Trump, the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, and Vladimir Putin of Russia.

The three-way meeting has been likened to a modern-day Yalta, when in 1945 then US president, Franklin D Roosevelt, Britain’s Winston Churchill and the Soviet leader Joseph Stalin met in Crimea to carve up Europe after the second world war. However, analysts say a “Yalta 2.0” that issues edicts on Ukraine and Taiwan without their involvement is unlikely.

Xiaoyu Pu, an associate professor at the University of Nevada’s department of political science, said a three-way meeting “might be more symbolic than substantive, although symbolism itself matters in great-power diplomacy.

“Trump appears interested in nuclear arms control and denuclearisation. Xi’s priority is likely to maintain a stable relationship with Washington while preserving China’s strategic partnership with Russia. Putin, meanwhile, wants to maintain his bargaining leverage over Ukraine.”

Apec begins on Wednesday and follows a flurry of other diplomatic gatherings, chief among them the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) meeting in the Philippines, starting on Sunday, which Trump will attend.

While he is in Manila, US officials will hold a sidelines meeting of the so-called Quad – Australia, India, Japan and the US. In addition, a Francophone summit, which includes the Canadian prime minister, Mark Carney, and the French president, Emmanuel Macron, is happening concurrently in Cambodia.

Critical global issues at all these summits will include AI supply chains and governance, tariff reductions and the green energy transition. Trump’s focus has been on denuclearisation. A key regional focus will be on the South China Sea, where Beijing has built massive military outposts in disputed waters, as well as the gigantic Indonesian forest fire haze blanketing the region.

The humanitarian and security crisis in Myanmar has become a perennial issue, as is status of Taiwan, which China does not recognise. The self-ruled island is sending the founder of the tech hardware company Acer as its envoy to Apec.

For well over a decade, Washington’s engagement in the region has been coloured by concern over the rise of China, with the then president Barack Obama telling Australia’s parliament in 2011 that the US was “a Pacific power, and we are here to stay” – a policy known as the “pivot to Asia”.

The strategy of intense diplomacy and trade deals was adopted by Joe Biden, who hoped to gather allies on the continent who wanted to balance their relationships with both superpowers.

Some analysts see Trump’s transactional style in Asia as a continuation of the pivot policy. Still, the president’s volatility and unpredictability have spooked smaller nations, who regard China as a more predictable partner.

Jayant Menon, an economist at Singapore’s Iseas-Yusof Ishak Institute, said that countries in south-east Asia had been “laying the groundwork for a future with reduced dependence on the United States” by seeking deals elsewhere, including with each other and the EU.

“Amid persistent uncertainty and an administration determined to levy tariffs by any legal means, south-east Asia’s ability to ride out another round of tariffs depends on deeper regional integration and diversification beyond the US market,” he wrote this month in the East Asia Forum.

Beijing’s more predictable policies do not necessarily make it any more palatable for its regional neighbours, however, and the Chinese premier, Li Qiang, could have tough talks when he goes to Manila for the Asean summit, especially on the South China Sea.

China’s top diplomat, Wang Yi, recently urged Manila to rein in Filipino politicians who he said had repeatedly “smeared” Beijing, after public outcries in the country over the disputed waters.

Brunei, China, Malaysia, the Philippines, Taiwan and Vietnam have been locked in decades-long territorial standoffs in the busy waterway, which could sit on deposits of oil and gas. Washington has sent military ships on “freedom of navigation operations”, despite Chinese outcries, although US-linked crises in Iran, Ukraine, Venezuela, and Palestine and Israel have sucked up Washington’s focus under Trump’s second term.

A decades-long attempt to sign a South China Sea pact is not expected to happen at this year’s Asean summit, as previously hoped. Despite monthly meetings, negotiations will have to continue next year, when Asean’s leadership rotates to Singapore.

Diplomats at the Asean summit will also be focusing on Myanmar, which is seeking reintegration. Each country in the group is assessing how to deal with their isolated neighbour. The timing of summits this week coincides with a judgment in the international court of justice on Thursday that could find Myanmar responsible for genocide. The coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, remains barred from Asean but is allowed to send a representative.

Asia’s week of diplomacy will be followed next month by the G20 summit in Miami, Florida, which Xi is expected to attend if US and Chinese relations continue to hold steady.
